# Detailed plan: cap tracked MCP tasks per server, unfinished ones included, through one way in

> Written on main `31c1357` (dev0, a team host), worktree `pmcp-338`, branch
> `plan/338-task-cap`. Every number below was measured on that tree, or on
> the spike of this plan applied to it (local branch `spike/338`, commit
> `6062cfe` (rev 12); rev 1's was `ffee47e`, rev 2's `3bb5b72`, rev 3's `812af01`,
> rev 4's `fd6c342`, rev 5's `efc9bb6`, rev 6's `e4e9044`, rev 7's `879b2ba`,
> rev 8's `212e9d6`, rev 9's `39cccd7`, rev 10's `fd740c0`, rev 11's `8ddb584`;
> never pushed). This PR carries only this file.
>
> **Revision 12 (2026-10-05), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 11
> on rev 11 (`44173de`): gemini agrees; claude, grok and codex disagree, each
> with one blocking finding, all in one class -- a task the cap moved into a
> teardown's custody was invisible to a reader that consulted only the
> registry. Claude's and grok's are the same finding (below); codex's is the
> same class on the request readers.
>
> **Codex F001 (blocking): custody release dropped context an in-flight poll
> needed.** `get_task` and `get_task_result_with_task` read only the
> registry, so a poll on a task held in a custody sent no context; when the
> teardown was then cancelled its custody, the only copy, went with it, and
> the reply and the next cancel kept `None`. Reproduced on rev 11 with
> codex's falsifier as written: `{'tasks/get': ([None, None], None),
> 'tasks/result': ([None, None], None)}`; main keeps `{'tenant': 'b'}`.
> **Fix:** `_pinned`, the one place a request takes its pin, puts a record
> held only in a custody back into the registry (`TaskRegistry.restore`),
> pinned first so the caps it re-applies cannot take it straight back, and
> only for the server's current connection. So a record a request is working
> on never lives only in a custody, and releasing a custody can drop only
> what nothing pins and no other teardown owes. `get_task_record` is read only
> under such a pin (structurally pinned). The teardown's own per-entry pin
> does the same, so its "fall back to the custody" branch is gone.
>
> **Claude/grok F001 (blocking): an owed task in a custody was invisible to every other
> reader.** `get_active_tasks` read only the registry, so once the cap moved
> an owed task into a teardown's custody, a concurrent forced disconnect
> neither owed nor cancelled it, a non-forced disconnect did not refuse on
> it, and a refresh did not count it. The owning teardown then found the
> connection gone and skipped it. Reproduced on rev 11 with the seat's
> falsifier as written: `((1, []), [('alpha', 'a1'), ('beta', 'f0'),
> ('beta', 'f1')])`, `b1` never cancelled; and its non-forced variant:
> `(True, 0, None)` instead of a refusal.
>
> **The class: two places a task can be active, readers consulting one.**
> Rev 12 makes `get_tracked_tasks` the single source (Design decision 18):
> the registry's records plus what live custodies hold that the registry
> gave up (`TaskRegistry.owed_records`), on the server's current connection.
> Every reader -- teardown snapshots, both disconnect gates, the refresh gate
> and its counts, restart counts -- already goes through it or
> `get_active_tasks`; a structural guard now pins that nothing else reads
> the registry or the custodies.
>
> **A record another teardown owes:** the second teardown owes it too and
> cancels it now, like any snapshot entry, never leaving it to an owner that
> may find the connection gone. Duplicate cancels are possible, as before.
>
> **The custody bound is restated, honestly.** The registry bound in live
> requests is unchanged. Custodies now inherit, so the tasks pmcp tracks only
> through custodies are bounded by the owed evictions of the current chain
> of overlapping teardowns: history within the chain, zero when none runs. In
> codex's handoff loop (no cancel ever answered) the newest custody owes
> 1,100 tasks, every one a real downstream task a disconnect was asked to
> cancel; the registry stays at 101. Design decision 18 names the trilemma.
>
> **Tests:** claude's and codex's falsifiers as written; claude's non-forced
> variant; a generated harness over every ordering of 7 alphabets drawn from
> overlapping forced refreshes, forced and non-forced disconnects, aborted
> teardowns, floods, `tasks/get` and `tasks/result` polls, a re-tracking
> listing and answered requests (2,880 orderings), checking every context
> sent and, at the end, that every task the model tracks is tracked with its
> context; restore tests (pinned first; current connection only);
> the connection filter; `owed_records` dedupe; the structural reader guard;
> the obligation bound in `_LiveBounds`. **Notes:** N1, the bound now reads
> `R(s) + 1` per server everywhere; the mutation runner now counts a kill
> only for pytest exit 1 with a failing test named, and the whole table was
> re-run under that rule.
>
> Rev 11 is `44173de`.
>
> **Revision 11 (2026-10-05), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 10
> on rev 10 (`14458cd`): gemini and claude agree (claude with two notes),
> grok was degraded, and codex has one blocking finding in two parts. Both
> parts reproduced natively with codex's falsifier, as written, on the rev 10
> spike: `beta_cancelled: False, result: (101, [])`, and `retained: 1100`
> with `R=0, T=1` against a stated bound of 101.
>
> **Codex F001, part 1: a forced refresh missed a task it was tracking when
> it started.** Rev 10 saved server names and read each server's snapshot
> only when its turn came, so while alpha's cancel awaited, a listing on
> beta evicted `beta::b` and the teardown never cancelled it.
>
> **Codex F001, part 2: the stated bound failed once requests finished.** The
> proof bounded a teardown's snapshot by R at the moment it was read, then
> used the current R; the test fixed R at its peak.
>
> **Why a lock-free rule fixes both** (Design decision 17). Under rev 9 and
> rev 10 a teardown *pinned* what it owed. With pins, "every task tracked at
> the request" and "bounded by requests in flight" cannot both hold: in the
> round-9 handoff loop each aborted teardown's successor owes one more record
> than it did. Rev 10 escaped by reading the snapshot late, which is part 1.
> Rev 11 takes the obligation out of the registry instead:
> - **A teardown fixes what it owes before its first await**: every active
>   record of every named server, read in its first statement.
> - **It holds it in a `TaskCustody`, not in pins.** The registry still
>   evicts an owed record, and `_evict` hands it to the custody, so it is
>   still cancelled. `_record_task` merges a write with the custody's record
>   when the registry no longer holds the key, so the newest context pmcp
>   holds survives an eviction while a teardown owes it.
> - **It pins only the entry whose `tasks/cancel` is in flight**, like
>   `cancel_task`: one request, one pin.
> - **No lock.** Nothing a teardown owes counts against the caps, so rev 10's
>   per-server lock has nothing left to do and is removed. That also answers
>   claude's N1 (`_teardown_locks` was never pruned) and N2 (a forced
>   disconnect could wait behind a forced refresh's other servers): there is
>   no lock to prune or to wait on.
>
> **The bounds, as two claims that hold at every instant:**
>
>     records of one server <= max(per_server, R(s) + 1)
>     all records           <= max(total, R + 1)
>
> with R the `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and `tasks/cancel` requests in flight
> *now*, a teardown's current cancel included (the sum of the pin counts).
> Separately, a teardown's custody is exactly its request-time snapshot,
> never more, so at most what the registry held for its servers then: a
> bound in R at its request, that is in the peak concurrency while it runs.
> Codex's shrinking-R ordering now retains 100 records with R = 0.
>
> **Tests:**
> - codex's falsifier, as written (`test_teardown_snapshot_acceptance`);
> - a generated test over all 180 orderings of {teardown, poll start, poll
>   end, flood on the other server, newer context, one cancel answered}, for
>   a forced refresh of both servers and a forced disconnect (360 runs). After
>   every `put`, `unpin` and `watch` it checks both claims with R read live,
>   and an independent model checks that every owed task is cancelled with
>   the newest context pmcp held;
> - codex's handoff loop and the 12 overlap variants, now with R read live
>   and the custody checked to never grow;
> - custody unit tests, a one-pin-at-a-time test, and custody release on
>   exception and cancellation (the autouse leak guard covers custodies too);
> - a structural guard: the snapshot is the helper's first statement, with
>   no await before it in the helper or its callers, and there is no lock.
>
> Rev 10 is `14458cd`.
>
> **Revision 10 (2026-10-05), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 9
> on rev 9 (`e1cfbe5`): claude agrees, grok and gemini have no blocking
> findings, and codex has one.
>
> **Codex F001: overlapping forced disconnects gave unbounded retention at
> fixed concurrency.**
> - Each teardown pinned its whole snapshot, including the overflow its
>   predecessor's pins had kept.
> - The next forced disconnect cancelled the predecessor's in-flight
>   request, then pinned the larger snapshot before the predecessor
>   released.
> - Codex measured 100 → 1,101 records after 1,001 handoffs. Rev 9's bound,
>   `max(cap, pinned + 1)`, still "held" only because `pinned` grew.
>
> **Mechanism: (b), forced teardowns are serialised per server** (Design
> decision 16). A teardown takes its server's lock and reads its snapshot
> *inside* it, after any earlier teardown of that server has ended and
> released. So a server never has two teardown snapshots pinned at once, and
> a snapshot never contains records that only another teardown's pins kept.
>
> **The bound is now a function of the caps and the requests in flight
> only.** Let R be the in-flight `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and
> `tasks/cancel` requests, and T the servers with a teardown in flight:
>
>     records of one server <= max(per_server, R + max(per_server, R + 1) + 1)
>     all records           <= max(total, R + T * max(per_server, R + 1) + 1)
>
> **Tests:**
> - codex's handoff loop is a test: 1,001 handoffs at the default caps,
>   peak at most 101. On rev 9's manager it fails at 102, measured.
> - a generated test: 2 or 3 overlapping teardowns × 0, 1 or 3 in-flight
>   get/result/cancel requests × the forced-disconnect or
>   `cancel_active_tasks` path, over 40 rounds with floods. It checks the
>   bound after every `put` and `unpin`. 6 of its 12 cases fail on rev 9.
> - a structural guard: the snapshot is read and pinned only inside the
>   teardown lock.
>
> **Claude's N1–N3:**
> - N1: the invariant is an explicit skip, not an `assert` (tested);
> - N2: the bound text states that past the total, a smaller server can give
>   up a record while larger ones are held only by pins;
> - N3: `_pinned` releases through an `ExitStack`, so one failing release
>   cannot leak the others (tested).
>
> Rev 9 is `e1cfbe5`.
>
> **Revision 9 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 8
> on rev 8 (`3857e6e`). The claude seat disagreed with one blocking finding;
> it reproduced the evidence and confirmed that isolation holds. Codex's one
> blocking finding is the same race in a third ordering:
> 1. poll A holds `old`;
> 2. an invocation supplies `new`, and poll B holds it;
> 3. a listing evicts the task;
> 4. A's reply restores `old`;
> 5. B's reply then discards its `new`.
>
> Pinning closes both, and both falsifiers are in the module as written.
> Both fail on rev 8's code, measured. Gemini found nothing blocking; grok
> was degraded.
>
> **F001: the round-7 race came back in the other order.** An older request
> captured OLD and awaited. A newer call stored NEW, and the cap then
> evicted that record. The reply found no record and put OLD back through
> rev 8's fallback. The next cancel sent `old` where main sends `new`.
> Measured with `context_race.py`: on rev 8, the `after-newer` order sends
> `old` for `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and `tasks/cancel`.
>
> **The degree of freedom is removed: a record with a request in flight
> cannot be evicted** (Design decision 15).
> - **Pinned at request start.** `get_task`, `get_task_result_with_task`
>   and `cancel_task` are decorated with `@_pins_task`. The teardown pins
>   its whole snapshot. `TaskRegistry` keeps a refcount per
>   `(server, task_id)`. `ClientManager._pinned`, a context manager, is the
>   only place pins are taken or released, so the release is always in a
>   `finally`.
> - **Eviction skips pinned keys.** When the last pin on a key goes, the
>   caps are re-applied.
> - **The bound:** a server holds at most
>   `max(per_server, its pinned records + 1)` records; the registry holds at
>   most `max(total, pinned records + 1)`. The pinned records number at most
>   one per in-flight get, result or cancel, plus each in-flight teardown's
>   snapshot.
>
> **Simplified, because pinning makes these unreachable:**
> - rev 8's `fallback_context` is removed;
> - rev 5's `stored_context` chain, and `_record_reply`, are removed;
> - the teardown's "record disappeared" branch is now an invariant.
>
> Every context test from rev 4, 5 and 8 still passes without them. Where a
> test had asserted that its pressure *evicted* the record, it now asserts
> that the pinned record *survived*. Mutants M77–M85 prove each pin and
> release is relied on.
>
> **Tests:**
> - the claude seat's falsifier, and codex's;
> - a teardown test: each record's *current* context is sent, not the
>   snapshot's;
> - a generated test over every held-context path × the newer call
>   before, during or after the await × the eviction before or after the
>   newer call, or after everything × the other records pinned at the cap
>   or not (90 cases), checking the bound at every `put` and `unpin`;
> - pin-release tests on reply, exception, cancellation and timeout (16
>   cases);
> - an autouse guard that fails any test leaving a pin behind;
> - a structural guard that every capturing request is pinned.
>
> **The computed-name limit of the resolution guard** is now stated in its
> docstring.
>
> Rev 8 is `3857e6e`.
>
> **Revision 8 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 7
> on rev 7 (`13740ee`): claude agrees, codex has one blocking finding, and
> gemini and grok are usable.
>
> **Codex F001: a late reply overwrote a newer context on the same
> connection.** `_record_reply` passed the context it captured before its
> await as `_record_task`'s *preferred* context. If a `gateway.invoke` on the
> same connection set a newer context for the same task meanwhile, the late
> reply put the old one back, and later cancels sent it. Main sends `new`;
> rev 7 sent `old`.
>
> Rev 8 fixes it with one precedence rule at the choke point. `_record_task`
> takes two required contexts:
> - `requestor_context`, the one a caller *supplies* with this request
>   (only `call_tool`), which wins;
> - `fallback_context`, the one pmcp *held* and captured before an await
>   (every reply), which fills in only when no same-connection record has a
>   context.
>
> So: supplied, else the existing record's, else the captured copy. Every
> caller in `CONTEXT_FLOW` is re-checked: now 13 entries, both parameters
> keyword-only with no default.
>
> **Measured with `context_race.py`:** with `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` or
> `tasks/cancel` as the older request, with or without an eviction, main and
> rev 8 send `new`; rev 7 sends `old`. Covered by:
> - codex's falsifier, as written;
> - a generated test: 5 held-context paths × {newer call lands first, reply
>   lands first} × {no eviction, evicted during the await}, 20 cases;
> - mutants M72–M74.
>
> **Claude's note: the resolution guard now follows helpers.** A plain helper
> (a `def`, or an `async def` with no await) that touches `_clients` or calls
> a resolver is itself a resolver, closed to a fixpoint. `_owns` is the one
> exception. Fixtures cover four helper shapes, the seat's included, and the
> `_owns` control. Mutant M75 is the seat's exact probe, and M76 makes the
> guard non-transitive.
>
> Rev 7 is `13740ee`.
>
> **Revision 7 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 6
> on rev 6 (`57881a8`): codex blocking ×2, claude partially agreeing (its one
> finding is codex's first), gemini OK, grok degraded.
>
> **The class, sixth round on isolation, with the degree of freedom
> removed:** a request captures its connection once, at entry. Every step of
> that request uses it: every send, every fallback, every `_record_task`, and
> every merge with existing state. Design decision 13.
> - **Codex F001: a late reply inherited the replacement's state.** In
>   `_record_task`, the merge with the existing record ran before the
>   ownership check. A's late reply took B's `created_at`, `tool_id` and
>   `requestor_context`. Now ownership is checked first: a reply from a
>   connection that is no longer the server's is built from the reply alone.
>   The merge also skips a record from another connection.
> - **Codex F002: an evicted task's result fallback crossed connections.**
>   The `tasks/get` fallback was bound to the record read at entry, which is
>   `None` after an eviction, so it resolved B by name. Now `_fetch_task`
>   takes the caller's captured connection and resolves nothing. The
>   fallback runs only while that connection is still the server's
>   (`_owns`); otherwise the result is returned with no task.
> - **Measured with `isolation.py`:**
>   - main and rev 6 return `(222.0, 'tasks::B', {'tenant': 'B'})` for A's
>     reply, send `tasks/get` to B, and pair A's result with `from-B`;
>   - rev 7 returns `(111.0, None, None)`, sends nothing to B, and gives
>     `task: None`.
>
>   Main has the same behaviour because it has no connection isolation at
>   all. This plan introduced isolation, and rev 7 closes it.
> - **Guards:**
>   - An AST guard derives the 8 request functions and requires that none
>     resolves a client by name after its first await. Only `_owns` may
>     look, and the two per-iteration resolvers are listed.
>   - A generated test drives every manager task operation on every reply
>     path (7), with the task tracked or evicted, and a replacement
>     registered mid-await. 14 cases; 12 fail on rev 6.
>   - The guard ties the generated table to the derived set.
>
> **Disk:** the spike, proofs, mutation copies and logs were kept on
> `/var/tmp` this round, and every mutation restore is sha256-verified.
> One mutation run overlapped another on the same tree by mistake. I
> stopped it, restored `manager.py` from the commit (checked with
> `git hash-object`), and re-ran the whole table in a single process chain;
> only that run is reported.
>
> Rev 6 is `57881a8`.
>
> **Revision 6 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 5
> on rev 5 (`20eca26`). The claude seat confirmed every round-4 fix: context,
> connection binding, `f001.py`, `codex_f001.py`, and 64 of 64 mutants. It
> found one blocking defect, the inbound mirror of codex's.
>
> **F001 (blocking): the replacement connection's task came back to the
> caller.** `gateway.invoke`, `gateway.tasks_result` and `gateway.tasks_list`
> looked the task up by `(server, task_id)` after their await. When a
> replacement connection tracked a same-id task, they returned it, possibly
> another tenant's. `invoke` also dropped the caller's own result. Measured
> with `readback.py`: rev 5 shows `from-B` on all three; main and rev 6 show
> `from-A`.
>
> **The fix is the class.** The manager returns the record it built from
> *this* reply. `TaskReply(result, task)` comes from the new
> `call_tool_with_task` and `get_task_result_with_task`; `call_tool` and
> `get_task_result` remain thin wrappers. The handlers use that record and
> never look a task up after an await. An AST guard derives every function
> that reads task state after an await, and compares it whole with a
> 15-entry allowlist that gives a reason for each. See Design decision 12.
>
> **Non-blocking:**
> - **Redaction scope:** `invoke`'s default redaction is back to main's rule,
>   "a task was requested and the reply carries one".
> - **Guards:** they now classify every `_send_request` method expression,
>   every `tasks/…` string, and every write to `_clients` by any means.
> - **`cancelled_task_count`:** see Consiliency/pmcp#349, in the CHANGELOG
>   and in Design decision 9.
>
> **Re-measured on the rev 6 spike `bd2838b`,** in a separate scratch
> worktree. The workspace volume was at 100% during this round; one file
> write was truncated and was restored from the spike commit:
> - `readback.py` on main, rev 5 and rev 6;
> - the module (122 cases), red on main (88 failed, 34 passed);
> - 73 mutants, all killed, plus a behavioural pass (26 of 29, with 3 argued);
> - the relevant modules on 3.10–3.12, and the full suite once;
> - the embedding proof on a fresh `31c1357`.
>
> Rev 5 is `20eca26`.
>
> **Revision 5 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 4
> on rev 4 (`d1cec3a`). The claude seat disagreed again, on the same context
> class one step earlier.
>
> **F001 (blocking), fixed at the choke point.** `get_task`,
> `get_task_result` and `_send_task_cancel` held the stored context across
> their await but recorded the reply without it. A task evicted in that
> window was re-tracked context-less, and later cancels went out without
> it. The seat's falsifier: rev 4 sent `{'taskId': 't', 'force': True}`;
> main sends the context.
>
> Rev 5 does not patch three sites. It makes `_record_task`'s
> `requestor_context` a required keyword-only parameter with no default, and
> records every post-await reply through one function, `_record_reply`, which
> requires the held context. The two functions that lead to it,
> `_fetch_task` and `_send_task_cancel`, require it too.
>
> A structural test inspects the four signatures and compares every call in
> `src/pmcp`, taken from the AST, against a 10-entry `CONTEXT_FLOW` table with
> a reason for each value. There are five per-path falsifiers plus the seat's,
> all red on rev 4's manager. Mutants M42a–M42i and M43 cover them. The
> `_record_task` calls in the three migrated test modules now pass
> `requestor_context=None` (and, for codex F001, `connection=None`). See
> Design decision 9.
>
> **Codex F001 (blocking; grok and gemini agreed): a teardown sent an old
> task's cancel and tenant context to a replacement connection.** It is
> fixed as one mechanism, Design decision 11:
> - every `ManagedClient` has a unique `connection_id`;
> - every record is stamped with the connection it came from.
>   `_record_task(..., connection=)` is required, and a reply from a
>   connection that is no longer the server's is not stored;
> - every `tasks/*` request goes through
>   `_task_client(server, bound_to=record)`, which refuses a record from a
>   previous connection;
> - the teardown checks the snapshot entry's connection before anything else.
>
> On codex's falsifier, rev 4 sent `tasks/cancel` for `b` with
> `{'tenant': 'old-tenant'}` to the replacement; main and rev 5 send it
> nothing. Two structural tests, both AST-derived, pin every `tasks/*` send
> and every binding (4 sends, 12 bindings), and pin that every connection
> registration drops the name's records first (3 paths).
>
> **Non-blocking:**
> - **`cancelled_task_count`:** worded exactly in the CHANGELOG, not
>   changed. The interface change is argued out of scope.
> - **"No connection that supports tasks"** is now the rule's name and text.
> - **Await-free async readers** join the scan's inline readers. The seat's
>   shape is a fixture, and mutants M44 and M45 cover it. Following returned
>   values was measured (28 or 93 sites by name collision) and rejected.
>
> **Re-measured on the rev 5 spike `efc9bb6`,** in a separate scratch
> worktree:
> - `f001.py` and `codex_f001.py` on main and on earlier revisions;
> - the module (112 cases), red on main (80 failed, 32 passed);
> - 64 mutants, all killed, plus a behavioural pass (18 of 19, with one
>   argued equivalent);
> - the 6 relevant modules on 3.10–3.12, and the full suite once;
> - the embedding proof on a fresh `31c1357`.
>
> Rev 4 is `d1cec3a`.
>
> **Revision 4 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** It answers board round 3
> on rev 3 (`14f1a04`). The claude seat disagreed, with one blocking finding
> and two non-blocking ones. It confirmed the single send path, `cancel_task`
> unchanged, the terminal-task, concurrent-disconnect and foreign-id
> handling, and the residual.
>
> **F001 (blocking), fixed as a class: a re-tracked record must not shadow
> the stored context.** The teardown sent `(current or snapped)
> .requestor_context`. A task evicted mid-teardown and then re-tracked by a
> concurrent `tasks/list` or `tasks/get` has a record with no context, and
> that record won. Measured with `f001.py`'s `re-tracked` row: rev 3 sent
> `b`'s cancel with context `None`, where main and rev 4 send
> `{'tenant': 't-1'}`.
>
> The rule now: a cancel carries the best context pmcp ever held for that
> `(server, task_id)`, which is the current record's, else the snapshot's.
> Every read of a stored context in `src/pmcp` is classified by a new
> structural test (5 sites). Only the teardown holds a record across an
> await. Design decision 9 argues why the fix is at that site and not in
> `put`.
>
> **The reconnect window (non-blocking) is now defined.** A snapshot task
> whose server has no live connection at that moment is skipped. Either a
> concurrent disconnect removed the server, or a reconnect has it listed but
> not connected. A skipped task is neither counted nor an error, whether or
> not it is still tracked. One check, `_task_client_unavailable`, is shared
> with `_task_client`.
>
> **N2b (non-blocking): the read scan is transitive.** A sync helper that
> reads, called before an await, counts as a read. The set of such helpers
> is a fixpoint over `src/pmcp`, pinned at five. The seat's helper shape, and
> a helper of a helper, are fixtures. The transitive scan finds four more
> sites, each of which writes and holds no record.
>
> **Re-measured on the rev 4 spike `fd6c342`,** built in a separate scratch
> worktree:
> - `f001.py` on main, rev 3 and rev 4;
> - the module (89 cases), red on main;
> - 41 mutants, all killed;
> - the 6 relevant modules on 3.10–3.12, and the full suite once;
> - the embedding proof on a fresh `31c1357`.
>
> Rev 3 is `14f1a04`.
>
> **Revision 3 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** Board round 2 on rev 2
> (`b8f3bcf`) found nothing blocking. Grok, codex and gemini agree; claude
> partially agrees, with two items, both resolved here.
>
> **N1: "a disconnect ends that task with the connection" was false for a
> remote server, and the decision is reversed.** For a remote server,
> `disconnect_server` only closes pmcp's transport, so rev 2's skip dropped
> a `tasks/cancel` that main sends. That was a behavioural regression for
> hosted tenant runs (`f001.py`: main `['a', 'b']`, rev 2 `['a']`).
>
> Rev 3 cancels an evicted snapshot task anyway, by the snapshot's
> `(server, task_id)` and stored `requestor_context`, through the one sender
> `_send_task_cancel`, which `cancel_task` now also uses. Rev 3 sends
> `['a', 'b']` on both triggers, as main does.
>
> The one residual left is a task evicted *before* a teardown starts. A
> remote server's copy of it keeps running, and this is now stated exactly
> in Design decisions 5 and 9 and in the CHANGELOG, README and SECURITY.
> Main's own `_remove_server_indexes` docstring made the same false claim,
> and it is corrected too. See Design decision 9.
>
> **N2: the snapshot scan now counts any reference to a read method as a
> read.** That covers an attribute (called, aliased or bound), a bare name,
> and a `getattr` string. The seat's alias, and six other forms, are
> fixtures. Mutant M36 (the alias in a real handler) and mutant M37 (rev 2's
> call-only guard) are both killed.
>
> **Re-measured on the rev 3 spike `812af01`:**
> - `f001.py` on main, rev 1, rev 2 and rev 3;
> - the module (76 cases), red on main;
> - 37 mutants, all killed;
> - the 6 relevant modules on 3.10–3.12, and the full suite once;
> - the embedding proof on a fresh `31c1357`.
>
> Rev 2 is `b8f3bcf`.
>
> **Revision 2 (2026-10-04), on main `31c1357`.** *(Its "skip" decision for
> an evicted snapshot task is superseded by revision 3.)* It answers the claude
> panel seat on PR 347: one blocking finding (F001) and three nits. Rev 1 was
> `f5ce6b5`; its spike was `ffee47e`.
>
> **F001 (blocking), fixed as a class.** A forced teardown snapshots the
> active tasks and then awaits one `tasks/cancel` at a time. Rev 1's cap can
> evict a record later in that snapshot while an earlier cancel is in
> flight. It has two triggers:
> - another caller records a task on that server;
> - the downstream answers `tasks/cancel` with a different task id. This one
>   repeats on every retry.
>
> The evicted record's cancel then said "Task not found", and the forced
> `disconnect_server` returned `(False, 0, 'Task not found: tasks::b')`, so
> `restart_server` and a forced `gateway.refresh` failed with it. Main
> returns `(True, 0, None)`. All three were measured with `f001.py` (*The F001
> repro*).
>
> The class is every function that reads tracked records and then awaits. It
> is derived from the code by an AST scan, which finds 10 sites. Design
> decision 9 gives each one a defined outcome for a record that is gone.
> - **The forced teardowns now cancel through one helper,
>   `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown`.** It re-reads each snapshot key before
>   its await and skips a record that is gone. *(Rev 3 changes "skips" to
>   "cancels by the snapshot".)*
> - **`get_task_result` reads its context once,** before any await.
> - **`cancel_task` is unchanged.**
>
> A structural test pins the site list. Another pins the helper as the only
> teardown caller of `cancel_task`.
>
> **The refresh corollary, verified and fixed.** The refresh loop's forced
> `disconnect_server` dropped its result. Main returned `ok: true` while the
> server stayed in `_clients` (`refresh_ignored.py`). Refresh now reports
> `Server '<name>' could not be disconnected: <reason>`.
>
> **Nits:**
> - **N1:** the CHANGELOG now says exactly what never evicts what: recording
>   a task never evicts that same task, and a `tasks/list` reply longer than
>   the cap keeps its last 100 tasks.
> - **N2:** the registry-write checks scan all of `src/pmcp`, and every
>   `.put(` call there is classified, so an alias cannot slip past.
> - **N3:** a key that is not a `(server, task_id)` tuple is a `KeyError`.
>   This includes `"st"`, which unpacks to `("s", "t")`.
>
> **Re-measured on the rev 2 spike:**
> - the repro, on main, rev 1 and rev 2;
> - the new module (67 cases), red on main;
> - 34 mutants, all killed;
> - the 6 relevant modules on 3.10–3.12, and the full suite once;
> - the embedding proof on a fresh `31c1357`.

## Task

Consiliency/pmcp#338. The claude seat found it while reviewing
Consiliency/pmcp#337, the #298 implementation, and it is pre-existing on
main.

- **The cap is shared.** pmcp keeps at most 100 *finished* task records, and
  that one cap covers every server. A downstream whose tasks a caller lists
  can fill it and evict another server's finished tasks.
- **Unfinished tasks are not capped at all.** A downstream can keep any
  number of records alive by reporting `status: "working"`. Since #298 the
  same is true of a status pmcp cannot use, which is now recorded as `null`
  and so is never "finished".

The issue suggests the fix: a per-server cap covering finished and unfinished
tasks, eviction within that server first, and a global ceiling as a backstop.
Eviction stays in record order, as #337 established.

It also asks for **three wording corrections** to #337's CHANGELOG entry.
They are verified against the code in *Documentation impact*.

The brief adds four requirements:
- derive every record path from the code, not from the issue;
- prefer one choke point that every path goes through over a check on each
  path;
- add a structural guard if feasible;
- prove the patches embed.

## Research summary

### Every path that records a task (derived from the code)

`ClientManager._tasks` is the only task store. On main it is a plain `dict`,
and the only line that inserts into it is in `_record_task`
(`manager.py:1813`, `self._tasks[(server_name, task_info.task_id)] = record`).
`McpTaskRecord(` is constructed only there (`manager.py:1796`); `rg
'McpTaskRecord\(' src/` has no other hit. So the record paths are exactly the
callers of `_record_task`, found with `rg -n '_record_task\(' src/`:

| # | Caller (`client/manager.py`) | Downstream request | Gateway tool that reaches it | What is recorded |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `call_tool` (`:3990`) | `tools/call` with `task` | `gateway.invoke` | the reply's `task` (or a top-level `taskId`) |
| 2 | `list_tasks` (`:4037`) | `tasks/list` | `gateway.tasks_list` | **every** listed task, one record each |
| 3 | `get_task` (`:4065`) | `tasks/get` | `gateway.tasks_get` | the reply |
| 4 | `get_task_result` (`:4090`) | `tasks/result` | `gateway.tasks_result` | the reply's `task`, when it has one |
| 4b | `get_task_result` → `get_task` (`:4092`) | `tasks/result`, then `tasks/get` | `gateway.tasks_result` | a reply without a `task` falls back to path 3 |
| 5 | `cancel_task` (`:4131`) | `tasks/cancel` | `gateway.tasks_cancel`, `disconnect_server(force)`, `cancel_active_tasks` | the reply |
| 5b | `cancel_task` (`:4123-4130`) | `tasks/cancel` | as 5 | pmcp builds a `cancelled` record itself when the reply has no task |

**Notifications are not a record path.** `_handle_downstream_notification`
(`:2157`) recognises exactly three methods, the three `*/list_changed`, and
returns `False` for anything else. `notifications/tasks/status` therefore
records nothing. The new module pins this
(`test_no_downstream_notification_records_a_task`).

**Nothing else writes.** The other uses of `_tasks` are reads
(`get_task_record`, `get_tracked_tasks`, `get_active_tasks`) and two removals:
- `_remove_server_indexes(drop_tasks=True)` (`:1643`) drops one server's
  records on a disconnect. Its five callers are `:1475`, `:2454`, `:2804`,
  `:3809` and `:3865`. The reconcile at `:2422` passes `drop_tasks=False`
  and keeps them.
- `self._tasks.clear()` (`:3741`) runs at shutdown.

### What hangs on a record (why eviction matters, and to whom)

Read from `manager.py` and `tools/handlers.py` on `31c1357`:

| Consumer | Uses the record for | When the record is gone |
|---|---|---|
| `cancel_task` (`:4109-4113`) | refuses without one | `(False, None, "Task not found: <server>::<id>")`. **Nothing is sent downstream.** |
| `disconnect_server` (`:1385-1398`) | refuses an unforced disconnect while `get_active_tasks(name)` is non-empty; a forced one sends `tasks/cancel` for each | not counted: not waited for, not cancelled |
| `cancel_active_tasks` (`:1865`) | same list | same |
| `get_task` / `get_task_result` (`:4051`, `:4076`) | reuse the stored `requestor_context` when the caller sends none | the downstream is still asked, without the stored context |
| `gateway.invoke` (`handlers.py:1664-1678`) | reads the record straight after `call_tool`; **default redaction is `task_info is not None`** | output is not redacted by default |
| `gateway.tasks_list` (`handlers.py:6021-6026`) | prefers the record | falls back to `McpTaskInfo(**task)` from the listing: no loss |
| `gateway.tasks_result` (`handlers.py:6118`) | `task` in the output | `task: null`; the result itself is unaffected |

Two consequences shape the design:
- **The record a path just stored must survive that same path.**
  `gateway.invoke` reads it back to choose its redaction. With "finished
  first", this is a real trap: a bucket of 100 `working` records receiving one
  `completed` would otherwise evict the new record, which is the only finished
  one.
- **An unfinished record carries behaviour, and a finished one carries a
  cache.** A finished record holds a final status the downstream still serves
  through `tasks/get`. An unfinished record is what `tasks_cancel`, the
  disconnect refusal and the stored `requestor_context` depend on.

### The repro (main `31c1357`, real `ClientManager` code)

The repro script, `repro.py`, is reproduced in *Verbatim bodies*. It drives `ClientManager.list_tasks` with
`_send_request` replaced by a canned reply, so every record goes through
main's real `_task_info_from_payload` → `_record_task` →
`_evict_terminal_tasks`. All caps are at their real defaults.

| Case | Input | main `31c1357` | patched (embedding proof tree) |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) cross-server | B records 2 finished; then A's `tasks/list` returns 98 / 99 / 100 finished | B keeps `['b0', 'b1']` / **`['b1']`** / **`[]`** | B keeps `['b0', 'b1']` in all three |
| (b) unfinished flood | F's `tasks/list` returns 500 `working` | **500** records kept | 100 kept (`w400`–`w499`) |
| (c) unusable status (#298) | N lists 500 with status `""`, `"   "`, `7`, `null`, `true` | **500** kept, every status `None` | 100 kept |

The boundary is what shows that the count is shared. With 98 from A, the
total of finished records is 100 and nothing goes. With 99, B's oldest goes.

Case (c) is why widening the "finished" set would not be enough. A status
pmcp cannot use is `None`, and `None` is neither finished nor known to be
unfinished. Only a cap that counts every record bounds it.

### The F001 repro (rev 2): main, rev 1 and rev 2

`f001.py`, in *Verbatim bodies*, runs the same scenario on three trees, each
extracted with `git archive`:
- records `b` and then `a` on one server, so the active-task snapshot (sorted
  by id) is `[a, b]` and `b` is the oldest record;
- sets a per-server cap of 2 where the tree has a cap;
- cancels `a` first, and records one more task while that cancel is in
  flight. With the concurrent-record trigger, another caller's `tasks/list`
  does it. With the foreign-task-id trigger, the downstream answers
  `tasks/cancel` with `other-a`.

| Tree | Trigger | `disconnect_server(force=True)` | `cancel_active_tasks()` | `tasks/cancel` sent for |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | either | `(True, 0, None)` | `(2, [])` | `['a', 'b']` |
| rev 1 `ffee47e` | either | **`(False, 0, 'Task not found: tasks::b')`** | **`(1, ['Task not found: tasks::b'])`** | `['a']` |
| rev 2 `3bb5b72` | either | `(True, 0, None)` | `(1, [])` | **`['a']`**: `b` skipped, so on a remote server it keeps running (panel round 2, N1) |
| rev 3 `812af01` | either | `(True, 0, None)` | `(2, [])` | `['a', 'b']`, `b` with its stored context |
| rev 4 (spike `fd6c342`) | either | `(True, 0, None)` | `(2, [])` | `['a', 'b']`, `b` with its stored context, as on main |

**The `re-tracked` row** (round 3's F001) records `b` and `a` with
`{'tenant': 't-1'}`. While `a`'s cancel awaits, a concurrent listing records
`x`, which evicts `b`, and then `b` again, with no context.

| Tree | `cancel_active_tasks()` | `b`'s cancel carries |
|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `(2, [])` | `{'tenant': 't-1'}` |
| rev 3 `812af01` | `(2, [])` | **`None`** |
| rev 4 `fd6c342` | `(2, [])` | `{'tenant': 't-1'}` |

The same script's `evicted-before` row is not F001. With a cap of 1, `b` is
evicted *before* the forced disconnect starts. Main tracks `['a', 'b']` and
cancels both. Rev 1, rev 2 and rev 3 track `['a']` and cancel `['a']`. That
is the cap's own residual (Design decision 9).

`refresh_ignored.py` replaces `disconnect_server` with one that returns
`(False, 0, "Error closing transport")`, then runs a forced
`gateway.refresh` that removes that server:

| Tree | `refresh` | `errors` | server still in `_clients` |
|---|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | **`ok: True`** | `None` | yes |
| rev 2 | `ok: False` | `["Server 'gone' could not be disconnected: Error closing transport"]` | yes, and now said |

### The test surface this touches

`rg -n '_max_terminal_tasks|\._tasks\[' tests/` on `31c1357` finds 7 sites,
all migrated in this plan:
- `tests/test_task_numeric_bounds.py:511, 549, 927, 944, 980` (#337's eviction
  tests). Three are single-server and move to `_tasks.per_server`. Two are
  cross-server and move to `_tasks.total`: the liar/honest test at `:549`
  and the slow-clock test at `:927`. Under the new rule, a per-server cap
  cannot make one server evict another's records at all, so the only knob
  where their claim still means something is the total. Both pass unchanged
  in substance there (see Design decision 4).
- `tests/test_client_manager.py:1914`, `test_terminal_task_records_are_evicted_past_cap`.
  It records 1 active and 20 finished records with a cap of 5. On main 5
  finished records and the active one survive. Now the cap counts the active
  one too, so 4 finished records survive (`done-16`–`done-19`) and the
  active one stays. The expectation changes; the test's point, "finished go
  first and the active one survives", does not.
- `tests/test_client_manager.py:4501` writes `manager._tasks[...] = record`
  directly. The registry has no `__setitem__`, so it becomes
  `manager._tasks.put(record)`.

`tests/test_tools.py` uses a `MockClientManager` with its own `tasks` dict and
is unaffected (measured: 196 passed).

## Design decisions (made explicitly)

### 1. One way in: `TaskRegistry.put`, a read-only mapping

The brief prefers removing the degree of freedom over checking each path.
Main already has a single writer (`_record_task`), but nothing keeps it
single. `_tasks` is a `dict`, so any new line `self._tasks[k] = v` would
bypass the cap silently, and mypy would accept it.

So `_tasks` becomes a `TaskRegistry`, in a new module
`src/pmcp/client/task_registry.py`:
- It is a `collections.abc.Mapping`: `[]`, `get`, `in`, iteration, `len`,
  `items`. Every existing reader keeps working unchanged.
- It has **no** `__setitem__`, `__delitem__`, `pop`, `popitem`, `setdefault` or
  `update`. A write that bypasses `put` is a mypy error (measured: `Unsupported target
  for indexed assignment`) and a `TypeError` at runtime.
- `put(record)` stores the record and applies both caps before it returns.
  The cap is not something a path can forget to call: storing *is* capping.
- `drop_server(name)` handles a disconnect and `clear()` handles shutdown.
  These are the two removals main already has.

`_record_task` stays the one function that builds a record and calls `put`.
It keeps its #298 field logic unchanged. The structural tests in Design
decision 7 pin both facts.

The registry is a class rather than a closure in the manager for three
reasons. It can be tested directly against a reference model
(`test_the_registry_matches_a_plain_restatement_of_the_rule`). Its mutable
surface is its type. And the manager loses the 30 lines of
`_evict_terminal_tasks`.

### 2. Per server: 100 records, finished and unfinished alike

Each server has its own bucket, capped at `per_server = 100`. That is main's
number: on main, 100 was the limit a single busy server could already reach
for finished tasks. A bucket over its cap gives up **its own** records and
nothing else, so no downstream can evict another's.

### 3. Victim order inside a server: finished first, then unfinished, each in record order

Decided: **finished first.** Inside the server being evicted from:
1. its oldest finished record (`completed`, `failed` or `cancelled`), if it
   has one;
2. otherwise its oldest unfinished record. Any other status counts as
   unfinished, including `None` and unknown future statuses.

"Oldest" means least recently recorded by pmcp. That is the record order
#337 established (`_recorded_order`, pmcp's own sequence). A re-record, such
as a `tasks_get` refresh, makes a task the newest again. No downstream
timestamp plays any part.

The argument comes from the consumer table in the research summary:
- A finished record is a cache. Losing it costs a caller nothing they cannot
  get back: `tasks_get` and `tasks_result` still reach the downstream, and
  `tasks_list` falls back to the listed payload.
- An unfinished record carries behaviour that silently stops when it goes:
  - `tasks_cancel` refuses without asking the downstream;
  - the disconnect refusal and the forced-disconnect cancel skip it;
  - the stored `requestor_context` is lost.
- Pure record order would let a burst of finished tasks push out older
  unfinished ones. That is exactly the work a caller may still need to
  cancel.

The cost: a server that holds a few long-running unfinished tasks and churns
many finished ones keeps its unfinished tasks longer than pure record order
would. That is the intent. It cannot be abused across servers, because the
bucket is the server's own. A server flooding its own bucket with unfinished
tasks only evicts its own records: after 500, it keeps the newest 100
(repro (b)).

**The record `put` just stored is never a victim of that `put`.** This is
needed by `gateway.invoke`'s redaction default (research summary). The
consequence: a bucket full of unfinished records that receives a finished one
evicts its oldest unfinished record, not the newcomer
(`test_the_record_just_stored_is_never_its_own_victim`,
`test_invoke_at_the_cap_keeps_its_record_and_redacts_by_default`).

### 4. The total: 1000 records, taken from the server holding the most

`total = 1000`, which is ten servers at their cap. It is a backstop for
memory: below it, the per-server caps decide everything. Past it, one record
goes:
- from **the server holding the most records**;
- on a tie, from the server whose next victim (by the rule in decision 3) was
  recorded first;
- never the record just stored.

Why this rule rather than "the globally oldest record":
- **The largest server pays first.** A server stays at or below the others'
  size, and so never loses a record to the total while a larger one exists.
  Before the total is reached, no server loses anything to another.
- **The two #337 cross-server tests keep their meaning under it.**
  - `test_one_downstream_cannot_keep_its_tasks_by_claiming_a_future_timestamp`:
    the liar has 2, the honest server 1, and the total is 2. The liar is
    the largest, so its oldest record, `b1`, goes.
  - `test_an_honest_server_with_a_slow_clock_keeps_its_newer_records`: one
    record each, total 1. The new record is never taken, so the on-time
    server's `a` goes.

  Both migrated to the total knob and pass without changing an assertion
  (measured).

The cost is an O(records) scan when the total is exceeded: one
`_candidate` pass per server. That is at most 1000 records, once per `put`
past the total. Below the total, `put` is O(bucket) ≤ 100.

The caps are attributes on the registry (`per_server`, `total`), not
configuration. Tests lower them. Making them operator-configurable is a
non-goal: no one has asked, and main had a constant. A cap below 1 is
refused with `ValueError`.

### 5. An evicted unfinished task: what the caller sees

Eviction forgets pmcp's record, not the downstream's task. Pinned by
`test_an_evicted_unfinished_task_is_re_tracked_by_tasks_get`:

| Caller action | Result |
|---|---|
| the call that created it (`gateway.invoke`) | already returned; its output was built while the record existed |
| `gateway.tasks_get` | still proxied to the downstream; the reply is recorded again, as the newest, which can evict another of that server's records |
| `gateway.tasks_result` | the same, through its `task` payload or its `tasks/get` fallback |
| `gateway.tasks_list` | lists it if the downstream does, and records it again |
| `gateway.tasks_cancel` | `ok=false`, `status: "not_found"`, `Task not found: <server>::<id>`, as for a task pmcp never saw. **Recovery:** call `gateway.tasks_get` first, then cancel (pinned) |
| `gateway.disconnect_server` | not counted as active: an unforced disconnect is not refused for it, and a forced one sends no `tasks/cancel` for it, because no snapshot holds it. **A stdio server's task ends with its process; a remote server's task keeps running** after the disconnect (rev 3, panel N1). Main's `_remove_server_indexes` docstring claimed "the downstream's tasks died with the connection", which is false for a remote server, and rev 3 corrects it. A record evicted *during* a forced teardown is different: the teardown still cancels it (Design decision 9) |
| a stored `requestor_context` | forgotten; the caller sends it again |

**Decided: `cancel_task` is unchanged.** It could forward an unknown task id
downstream instead of refusing. But that changes what `tasks_cancel` means
for *every* unknown id, not only evicted ones, and pmcp cannot tell "evicted"
from "never seen" without keeping tombstones, which is a second unbounded
structure. The recovery is one call, and the README now says so.

### 6. Disconnect, reconcile and shutdown keep main's semantics

- A **disconnect** drops all of that server's records: `drop_server(name)`
  replaces main's loop over every key at `:1644`. It also frees the
  server's share of the total (`test_a_disconnect_drops_only_that_servers_records_and_frees_its_count`).
- A **reconcile** (`drop_tasks=False`) keeps the records. This is main's
  existing test, `test_reconcile_preserves_tracked_task_records`, migrated
  to `put`.
- **Shutdown** clears the registry (`clear()`).
- A reconnect under the same name starts with an empty bucket, because every
  reconnect path goes through `_remove_server_indexes(name)` first.

### 7. Structural guards: one way in, a closed list of record paths

These are AST or type checks over `src/pmcp`, in the new module. Rev 2 adds
two more, for the read-then-await class, in Design decision 9.
- `test_record_task_is_called_by_exactly_the_tested_paths`: the set of
  functions that call `_record_task` must equal the set named in the
  per-path test table, `RECORD_PATHS`. A new record path fails this until it
  is added to that table, which also gives it a cap test (mutant M20).
- `test_only_record_task_builds_records_and_puts_them` scans **all of
  `src/pmcp`** (rev 2, N2):
  - `McpTaskRecord(` is constructed only in `_record_task`;
  - every `.put(` call in the package is classified, by file, function and
    receiver. There are exactly two: `self._tasks` in `_record_task`, and the
    npm resolver's `self._queue` in `_pump`.

  So an alias (`registry = self._tasks; registry.put(...)`) fails here, and
  so does any new `.put(`, until someone classifies it. That is an
  allowlist, not a denylist: an unrelated new queue costs one line in the
  test. Mutants M17, M19 and M35.
- `test_the_registry_is_written_in_exactly_three_places`: every use of
  `_tasks` in `src/pmcp` that is not a plain read (`get`, `items`, `[]`,
  `in`) must be one of four:
  - the binding in `__init__`;
  - `put` in `_record_task`;
  - `drop_server` in `_remove_server_indexes`;
  - `clear` in `_disconnect_all_unlocked`.

  `_tasks` is never passed or assigned as a value, so it has no alias
  (mutant M21).
- `test_the_registry_cannot_be_written_or_replaced_around_put`:
  - `TaskRegistry` has no mutating dunder or dict method;
  - an item assignment raises `TypeError`;
  - `self._tasks` is bound only in `__init__`, so nothing swaps in a dict
    (mutant M21).
- `test_every_record_path_is_capped` (parametrised over `RECORD_PATHS`,
  seven cases, one per row of the record-path table): each path is driven
  through the real manager method into a bucket already at its cap of 2.
  The test asserts:
  - that server's oldest record went;
  - the new record is present;
  - the other server's record is untouched.

mypy is a fifth guard, for free: a `self._tasks[...] = ...` anywhere in `src/`
is a type error once `_tasks` is a `Mapping`.

### 8. What is not changed

- the #298 field logic in `_record_task`: `created_at` stickiness,
  `unusable_fields` and `updated_at`;
- `_task_info_from_payload`;
- every handler except `refresh` (rev 2, Design decision 9);
- `cancel_task`'s and `gateway.tasks_cancel`'s answers (rev 3 splits the
  sending half of `cancel_task` into `_send_task_cancel`, with the same
  behaviour);
- the public output models. `_recorded_order` stays the private attribute
  #337 added; the registry now stamps it.

### 9. Every read-then-await site has a defined outcome for a gone record (rev 2, F001)

**The class.** Rev 1's cap can remove a record that some code read before an
await and uses after it. The ways a record goes:
- eviction, when anyone records a task on that server;
- eviction, when the downstream answers `tasks/cancel` with a different
  task id;
- a disconnect, which drops it.

Main had only the third, so a site that main got away with can now fail.

**Derived from the code.** The list below is computed by an AST scan,
`test_every_snapshot_then_await_site_is_classified`. It takes every function
in `src/pmcp` that reads tracked records at a source position before the end
of one of its `await`s. A read is **any reference** to `get_active_tasks`,
`get_tracked_tasks`, `get_task_record` or `_tasks` (rev 3, N2):
- an attribute, whether it is called, aliased
  (`active = cm.get_active_tasks`) or passed as a bound method;
- a bare name;
- a string constant, as in `getattr(cm, "get_active_tasks")`.

Rev 2 counted only a direct call, so the seat's
`active = self._client_manager.get_active_tasks; snapshot = active(); await
...` passed it. `test_the_snapshot_scan_sees_every_way_of_reading` runs the
scan on seven fixture forms, the seat's included. Each must be found, and
the same function without the read must not be. Mutant M36 puts the seat's
alias into a real handler; mutant M37 puts rev 2's call-only predicate back
into the guard. Both are killed. An `await f(read())` counts, because
the read happens before that await resumes. It also takes any function with
a read and an await in the same loop, where the read precedes the next
iteration's await. The scan finds exactly these 13 as of rev 6 (rev 5 had 14). `tasks_list` is gone, and `get_task_result` is now `get_task_result_with_task`. The test asserts that
the set equals `SNAPSHOT_SITES`, so a new site fails until it is classified
(mutant M34).

**Await-free async readers (rev 5, round-4 N2c).** The round-4 seat measured
a second dodge: an `async def` helper that reads and returns without
awaiting. Its body runs to completion inside the caller's `await`, just as a
sync helper does, so `_sync_readers` now includes an `async def` that reads
and has no await.

The alternative was to follow every async reader's *returned* value into its
callers. It was measured and rejected. Matching by name, it flags 28 sites
when it follows async functions that read after their last await, and 93
when it follows every async reader. Common names such as `refresh`,
`call_tool` and `main` collide across `src/pmcp`, so the table would stop
meaning anything. An async function that suspends is still scanned as a site
of its own.
- The seat's shape is a fixture, `await-free-async-helper`.
- Mutant M45 puts the seat's exact pair into `GatewayTools`.
- Mutant M44 drops the async clause from the guard.

Both mutants are killed. The fixpoint over `src/pmcp` is now six functions:
`_record_reply` joins the five (rev 5).

**Transitive (rev 4, N2b).** The seat measured a dodge: a sync helper that
reads, called by an async function that then awaits, while the caller names
no read method itself. The scan now computes `_sync_readers`:
- the plain `def` functions in `src/pmcp` that reference a read;
- closed to a fixpoint over references to one another;
- with `__init__` excluded, since it binds `_tasks` and does not read it.

A reference to any of them counts as a read. On today's tree the set is
exactly `get_task_record`, `get_tracked_tasks`, `get_active_tasks`,
`_record_task` and `_remove_server_indexes`, pinned by
`test_the_sync_readers_in_src_are_the_expected_five`.

An `async def` reader is deliberately not included. Its reads happen inside
its own body, after its caller's `await` has started, and that body is
scanned as a site of its own. Counting a call to it as a read would flag
every `await self._client_manager.call_tool(...)` falsely. The fixtures add
the seat's shape (`_active_snapshot`) and a helper of a helper.
- Mutant M40 puts the seat's exact helper pair into `GatewayTools`, and the
  classification test catches it.
- Mutant M41 makes the guard non-transitive, and the fixtures catch it.

| # | Site | Reads | Then awaits | Outcome for a record gone by then | Test (both triggers unless noted) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `manager.disconnect_server` | `get_active_tasks(name)` | the helper | **still cancelled** (rev 3), by the snapshot's id and stored context; `(True, n, None)` | `test_forced_cancel_loop_survives_concurrent_records` (the seat's falsifier) |
| 2 | `manager.cancel_active_tasks` | `get_active_tasks()` | the helper | **still cancelled**, and counted | `test_cancel_active_tasks_cancels_a_record_evicted_mid_loop` |
| 3 | `manager._cancel_tracked_for_teardown` (new) | `get_task_record` per snapshot entry | `_send_task_cancel` | re-reads before each await. Finished meanwhile: counted, nothing sent. Server with no live connection (removed, or reconnecting): skipped, not counted, not an error (rev 4). Otherwise sent, with the current record's context, else the snapshot's (rev 4) | `test_a_teardown_counts_a_task_finished_meanwhile_and_sends_nothing`, `test_a_teardown_skips_a_task_whose_server_has_no_live_connection` (removed / reconnecting / tracked-reconnecting), `test_teardown_cancel_keeps_the_snapshot_context` (the seat's falsifier) |
| 4 | `manager.get_task` | `get_task_record` | `_fetch_task` (`tasks/get`) | the context is read before the await, sent, and (rev 5) written back with the reply through `_record_reply` | `test_get_task_records_the_reply_if_evicted_mid_call`, `test_a_reply_recorded_after_an_eviction_keeps_the_held_context[tasks/get]` |
| 5 | `manager.get_task_result` | `get_task_record` | `tasks/result`, then `_fetch_task` | the context is read once, before any await. The reply's record and the fallback both get it (rev 5) | `test_get_task_result_keeps_the_stored_context_if_evicted_mid_call`, `…keeps_the_held_context[tasks/result]` and `[tasks/result->tasks/get]` |
| 6 | `manager.cancel_task` | `get_task_record` | `_send_task_cancel` | unchanged for the caller: the record is read before the await. The reply is recorded again (without `tool_id`), with the stored context (rev 5) | `test_cancel_task_records_the_reply_if_evicted_mid_call`, `…keeps_the_held_context[tasks/cancel]` |
| 7 | `handlers.refresh` | `get_active_tasks()` (a count) | `cancel_active_tasks`, each `disconnect_server`, `connect_all` | counts only; cancels via site 2; **a failed forced disconnect is now reported** | `test_forced_refresh_survives_a_record_evicted_mid_teardown`, `test_forced_refresh_reports_a_server_it_could_not_disconnect` |
| 8 | `handlers.disconnect_server` | `len(get_active_tasks)` before and after | `disconnect_server` | counts only. `cancelled_task_count` is "active tasks no longer tracked" (before − after), as on main, where a successful disconnect drops every record anyway | `test_gateway_disconnect_server_survives_a_record_evicted_mid_teardown` |
| 9 | `handlers._restart_resolved_server` | the same | `restart_server` | the same; it goes through site 1 | `test_restart_survives_a_record_evicted_mid_teardown` (via `manager.restart_server`) |
| 10 | ~~`handlers.tasks_list`~~ (rev 6: removed) | — | — | rev 6: it no longer reads the registry; it uses the listed records `list_tasks` built from the reply | `test_tasks_list_never_shows_the_replacements_record` |
| 11 | `manager.list_tasks` (rev 4, found transitively) | `_record_task` in a loop | the next server's `tasks/list` | records each task and dumps it at once; holds no record across an await | `test_every_record_path_is_capped[tasks/list]` |
| 12 | `manager._connect_stdio` (rev 4) | `_remove_server_indexes` | the spawn | a write (drops the name's records before a new connection); holds no record | the existing connect tests |
| 13 | `manager._connect_remote_stream` (rev 4) | the same | the transport | the same | the same |
| 14 | `manager.adopt_process` (rev 4) | the same | the handshake | the same | the same |

Rows 1, 2, 7, 8 and 9 run with three triggers: concurrent record, foreign
task id, and (rev 4) evicted then re-tracked without a context. Row 5 does
too.

Rev 6: `handlers.tasks_list` has left the table, and `handlers.invoke` and
`handlers.tasks_result` are no longer "safe readers after their last
await". None of the three reads task state after an await any more. They use
the record built from the reply (Design decision 12), and a separate guard
pins that no function reads task state after an await unless it is listed.

**The design removes the degree of freedom: one sender.** Rev 1 had two
teardown loops, each calling `cancel_task` and treating its refusal as
fatal. Rev 3 has one sender and one teardown helper:
- **`_send_task_cancel(server, task_id, force, requestor_context)`** is the
  only code that sends `tasks/cancel`; it records the reply. `cancel_task` is
  now its record check, "already terminal" or "Task not found", followed by
  `_send_task_cancel`. Its user-facing behaviour is unchanged (every existing
  `tasks_cancel` test passes).
- **`_cancel_tracked_for_teardown(snapshot)`** is the only code a teardown
  cancels through. `disconnect_server` and `cancel_active_tasks` pass it the
  active records they read. It does not call `cancel_task`, so "not found"
  cannot arise in a teardown at all.

`test_every_tasks_cancel_goes_through_the_one_sender` asserts three things:
- `_send_task_cancel` is called by exactly `cancel_task` and the helper;
- `cancel_task` is called only by `gateway.tasks_cancel`;
- the string `"tasks/cancel"` appears only in `_send_task_cancel`.

Mutants M25 and M26 restore rev 1's loops; both are killed.

**Rev 3 decision: an evicted snapshot task is cancelled, not skipped.** This
reverses rev 2. The panel's claude seat (round 2, N1) showed that rev 2's
reason, "the downstream's task dies with the connection", is false for a
remote server:
- `disconnect_server` only closes pmcp's transport (`_close_remote_transport`);
- no `tasks/cancel` goes out other than the cancel loop's.

A hosted tenant run on a remote server kept running after a forced
disconnect or restart that main would have cancelled. `f001.py` shows it:
main sends `tasks/cancel` for `['a', 'b']`, rev 2 for `['a']`. That is a
behavioural regression, not a wording problem.

So the helper cancels by the snapshot it was given. For each entry, after
re-reading:
- **tracked and finished meanwhile:** counted, nothing sent. This is the
  same as `cancel_task`'s "already terminal".
- **its server has no live connection** (rev 4 widens rev 3's "removed"):
  skipped; see "The no live connection rule" below.
- **otherwise, tracked or evicted:** `tasks/cancel` is sent through
  `_send_task_cancel`, with the current record's `requestor_context`, or
  the snapshot's when the record is gone *or has none* (rev 4).

**The context rule (rev 4, round-3 F001), fixed as a class.** A cancel
carries the best `requestor_context` pmcp ever held for that
`(server, task_id)`: the current record's if it has one, else the
snapshot's. Rev 3 sent `(current or snapped).requestor_context`. A record
evicted mid-teardown and then re-tracked by a concurrent `tasks/list` or
`tasks/get` exists, so it won, and it has no context, because neither path
passes one to `_record_task`. A context-scoped downstream can then refuse
the cancel. That would bring back round 1's failure (the refusal raises out
of the teardown) or round 2's (the run keeps going).

*Rev 4's audit was one step short (round-4 F001).* It classified every read
of a stored context and concluded that only the teardown held one across an
await. That was false:
- `get_task`, `get_task_result` and `_send_task_cancel` each read the stored
  context before their await, but recorded the reply *without* it.
- If the cap evicted the record during that await, the re-recorded task had
  no context.
- Every later cancel of it then went out without one, whether from a forced
  teardown or from `gateway.tasks_cancel` called with no context.

The seat's falsifier sent `{'taskId': 't', 'force': True}` on rev 4; main
sends the context. Fixing these three sites as well would have been the
fourth patch to this class in four rounds.

**Rev 5 removes the degree of freedom at the choke point.**
- **`_record_task(..., *, requestor_context)` is required, keyword-only, and
  has no default.** Every caller must pass the context it holds for the
  task, or a `None` it can justify. Its merge is unchanged: what the caller
  passes, else the existing record's.
- **One function, `_record_reply`, records a reply received after an
  await.** It takes the context pmcp held before the await, `stored_context`,
  as a required keyword. It writes that context back *unless the reply
  names a different task*; in that case the context is not that task's.
- **The functions that lead to `_record_reply` take the held context as a
  required keyword too.** They are `_fetch_task` (split out of `get_task`, so
  that `get_task_result`'s fallback hands on the context it read rather than
  re-reading a possibly evicted record) and `_send_task_cancel`.

So "held across an await" no longer matters for context: whatever a
function held is always written back.

`test_every_record_write_passes_the_context_its_caller_held` makes this
structural:
- It inspects the four signatures: keyword-only, no default.
- It collects every call to the four functions in `src/pmcp` from the AST,
  as `(callee, caller, value)`, and compares that set whole against
  `CONTEXT_FLOW`. That table has 10 entries, each with its reason.
- The two `None`-shaped entries are justified:
  - `list_tasks`: a listing does not attribute a context to a task. A
    tracked task keeps its own through the merge; an evicted one had already
    lost it, which is the stated residual.
  - `_record_reply`'s other-task branch.
- `cancel_task` passes the tracked record's context, not the caller's
  override, as main kept it.

Every path that records a reply after an await has a falsifier, in
`test_a_reply_recorded_after_an_eviction_keeps_the_held_context`:
`tasks/get`, `tasks/result`, the `tasks/result` → `tasks/get` fallback,
`tasks/cancel`, and the teardown cancel. In each, `t` is evicted during the
first request's await. The test asserts that the reply's record keeps the
context, and that the next cancel sends it. All five fail on rev 4's
manager, measured, and so does the seat's falsifier as written,
`test_get_task_keeps_the_context_it_held_if_evicted_mid_call`.
`test_a_reply_naming_another_task_does_not_take_this_tasks_context` pins the
other-task guard.

The mutants are M42a–M42g, one per caller, each dropping its held context to
`None`; M42h, which removes the other-task guard; M42i, where
`_record_reply` writes nothing; and M43, which gives `requestor_context` its
default back.

`test_every_read_of_a_stored_context_is_classified` (rev 4) stays, with
the same five reads.

*Why not in `put` (rev 4's argument, still true).* `put` stores whole
records built by `_record_task`, the one builder. After an eviction, nothing
the registry can see holds the context, so a merge in `put` could not
recover it. The callers hold it; rev 5 makes them hand it over.

**The "no connection that supports tasks" rule (rev 4; worded exactly in
rev 5).** A snapshot task whose server cannot be sent a task request at
that moment is skipped. No `tasks/cancel` is sent, the helper does not count
it, and it is not an error. That covers three cases:
- a concurrent disconnect removed the client;
- a reconnect has the client listed but not yet connected;
- the server no longer advertises tasks.

This applies whether the task is still tracked or was evicted. The check is
`_task_client_unavailable`, the same predicate `_task_client` raises on, so
the two cannot drift. Rev 3 skipped only a removed server. A reconnecting
one raised "not connected" out of the forced teardown, and
`handlers.disconnect_server` has no `try`.

*Why skip, not fail.* A forced teardown that fails on a task it cannot reach
is F001's shape again. A retry cannot succeed while the reconnect is in
progress either. For a tracked task the record stays, so `gateway.tasks_get`
and `gateway.tasks_cancel` reach it once connected. Main differs: for a
tracked record, `cancel_task` raised. The CHANGELOG states the residual: on
a remote server that is reconnecting, such a task is not cancelled.

Pinned by `test_a_teardown_skips_a_task_whose_server_has_no_live_connection`
(removed, reconnecting, tracked-reconnecting), and by mutants M28b (no skip)
and M39 (rev 3's narrower rule).

*`cancelled_task_count` (rev 5, round-4 non-blocking): worded, not
changed.* `gateway.disconnect_server` and `gateway.restart_server` report
`cancelled_task_count` as the active tasks that are no longer tracked after
the call, before minus after, as on main. A skipped task is therefore
included, though no cancel was sent for it. Reporting the helper's own count
instead would change the return types of `ClientManager.disconnect_server`
and `restart_server`, which are 3-tuples. Six test doubles implement them
(`test_tools`, `test_fresh_operator_baseline`, `test_package_identity_gate`,
`test_trust_boundaries_e2e`, `test_trust_boundaries_composition`,
`test_pkgid_panel_fixes`), and the handlers consume them. That is an
interface change beyond #338. The CHANGELOG now states the field's meaning
exactly. The interface change is tracked separately: see
Consiliency/pmcp#349.

**Rev 3 and rev 4 reproduce main's outcome on both triggers:** `(True, 0, None)`,
`(2, [])`, and `tasks/cancel` sent for `['a', 'b']`, with `b`'s stored
context (*The F001 repro*). Rev 2's reasons against this do not hold:
- **"A second sender."** There is still exactly one: `cancel_task` and the
  helper share `_send_task_cancel`. Only the record check, which is the
  user-facing answer, stays in `cancel_task`.
- **"Nothing changes for a disconnect."** That is false for a remote server.
- **"Design decision 5."** That row concerns a task evicted *before* a
  teardown, which no snapshot holds. It is now stated exactly, and stays a
  residual (below).

**The residual that remains, stated exactly.** It is a task evicted *before*
a forced teardown starts. pmcp no longer tracks it, so no snapshot holds it,
and the teardown cannot cancel it. A stdio server's task ends when pmcp
terminates the process. **A remote server's task keeps running** after the
disconnect. `f001.py`'s `evicted-before` row shows it: with a cap of 1,
rev 3 sends `tasks/cancel` for `['a']` and main for `['a', 'b']`.

This is the price of any bound: pmcp cannot cancel what it no longer
remembers, and remembering it is the unbounded structure #338 removes. A
bounded tombstone list would just move the cap. The CHANGELOG, README and
SECURITY all name it, and all give the remedy: `gateway.tasks_get`, then
`gateway.tasks_cancel`.

**The refresh corollary (verified).** The loop at `handlers.py:2107` (rev 2
numbering) called `disconnect_server(name, force=True)` and dropped the
result. Measured on main with `refresh_ignored.py`:
- a forced disconnect that returns `(False, 0, "Error closing transport")`,
  the transport-close failure path main already has;
- gave `refresh ok: True errors: None`;
- with the server still in `_clients`.

The server is one that refresh decided to remove: it was deleted from the
config, denied by policy, or its config changed. **Decided: report it, and
keep going.** Each failure appends `Server '<name>' could not be
disconnected: <reason>` to `errors`, so `ok` is false. The other servers are
still disconnected and connected; one stuck server does not abort the rest.
The reason text is what `gateway.disconnect_server` already returns to the
same caller, so nothing new is exposed. Rev 2 measures
`refresh ok: False errors: ["Server 'gone' could not be disconnected: Error
closing transport"]` (mutants M30 and M31).

### 10. `TaskRegistry` keys (rev 2, N3)

`__getitem__` raises `KeyError` for anything that is not a 2-tuple. That
covers a string: `"st"` would otherwise unpack to `("s", "t")` and *find* a
record. It also raises `KeyError` for a tuple with an unhashable part. So
`get` and `in` keep the `Mapping` contract: `None` and `False`, never
`ValueError` or `TypeError` (`test_a_key_that_is_not_a_pair_is_absent_not_an_error`,
six keys; mutants M32 and M33).

### 11. Task state is bound to the connection it came from (rev 5, codex F001)

**The finding.** `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown` checked availability by
server *name*. While an earlier cancel awaited, a reconnect could:
- clean up `beta`, dropping its records;
- register a replacement client for `beta`, possibly another URL or tenant.

The helper then sent the snapshot's `taskId: b` with
`{"tenant": "old-tenant"}` through the replacement. That leaked the old
context, and it could cancel an unrelated task with the same id. Main sends
the replacement nothing. Measured with `codex_f001.py` (*Verbatim bodies*):

| Tree | `cancel_active_tasks()` | sent to the replacement |
|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `(1, ['Task not found: beta::b'])` | nothing |
| rev 4 `fd6c342` | `(2, [])` | **`tasks/cancel` `{'taskId': 'b', 'task': {'requestorContext': {'tenant': 'old-tenant'}}, 'force': True}`** |
| rev 5 `efc9bb6` | `(1, [])` | nothing |

**The class.** Every outbound task request that pmcp derives from stored
state, a task id or a context, must go to the connection that state came
from. Every record pmcp keeps must come from the server's *current*
connection.

**One mechanism, enforced at the funnel.**
- **`ManagedClient.connection_id`** is unique for the process lifetime,
  taken from an `itertools.count`. `id()` is not used, because a later
  object can reuse it. A reconnect makes a new `ManagedClient`, so the new
  connection gets a new id.
- **`McpTaskRecord._connection_id`** (private) is stamped by
  `_record_task(..., connection=)`. That parameter is keyword-only and
  required. If `connection` is no longer `self._clients[server]`, the record
  is built and returned but **not stored**: a reply from the old connection
  must not become state of the new one. `None` (an unbound record) exists
  for tests that seed the registry; every call in `src/pmcp` passes the
  connection it sent on.
- **`_task_client(server, *, bound_to)`** is the only way to get a
  connection for a `tasks/*` request. `bound_to` is keyword-only and
  required. Through `_task_client_unavailable`, it refuses a record whose
  `_connection_id` differs from the server's current connection, with
  `Server <name> was reconnected; this task belongs to its previous
  connection`.
- **The teardown checks the snapshot entry's binding first,**
  `_task_client_unavailable(server, bound_to=snapped)`, before the terminal
  check and before the send. A mismatch is "server gone": skipped, not
  counted, not an error. It sends with `bound_to=snapped`.

**Every send site, derived from the AST.**
`test_every_task_request_is_bound_to_the_connection_its_state_came_from`
finds every `_send_request(…, "tasks/…")` in `src/pmcp`. Each must get its
connection from exactly one `_task_client(…, bound_to=…)` call in the same
function. `TASK_SENDS` lists the four:
- `list_tasks` → `tasks/list`, with `None`: a listing is the caller's
  query, built from no stored state. Its cursor comes from the caller.
- `_fetch_task` → `tasks/get`, with `bound_to`.
- `get_task_result` → `tasks/result`, with `record`.
- `_send_task_cancel` → `tasks/cancel`, with `bound_to`.

The test also collects every call that passes a binding on (`bound_to=` or
`connection=`) and compares them, as `(callee, caller, value)`, with the 12
entries in `BINDINGS`. It inspects the six binding parameters: keyword-only,
no default. `tools/call` is not a `tasks/*` send. It creates the task and
records it with `connection=managed`, the connection it called.

**Replacement paths drop the old records.**
`test_every_connection_replacement_drops_the_names_records_first` finds
every write to `_clients`, by any means (rev 6, round-5 non-blocking):
- item assignment and `del`;
- `update`, `setdefault` and `__setitem__`;
- `pop`, `popitem` and `clear`;
- binding the attribute itself.

It compares them whole with `CLIENT_WRITES` (9 writes). Every function that
*registers* a connection must really drop the name's records **on every path
before the registration**: `_cleanup_client`, or `_remove_server_indexes`
without `drop_tasks=False`.

Rev 6 checks this by dominance over the statement structure, not by line
order. An `if` counts only when both branches drop, a `try`/`with` counts
when its body drops, and a loop never counts. A drop on one branch only
(`_connect_stdio`'s `else`) therefore no longer passes by sitting on an
earlier line.
- Mutant M64 registers through `setdefault()` with no drop.
- Mutant M64b uses a drop that keeps the records.
- Mutant M56 removes the `else` drop.

All three are killed by this test. M56 and M64b are also killed
behaviourally by `test_a_stdio_connect_drops_the_names_records_before_anything_else`
(both branches): a connect drops the name's records first, even when it
then fails. M64 is a guard mutant: it changes no behaviour. On today's tree,
the registering functions are the ones that use `self._clients[...] = ...`.
There are three: `_connect_stdio`, `_connect_remote_stream` and
`adopt_process`. Each must call `_cleanup_client` or `_remove_server_indexes`
earlier in its body. That covers records that existed at the replacement.
The `_record_task` check covers a reply that arrives afterwards, and the
`_task_client` binding covers a snapshot or record held across it.
Mutant M56 removes the drop on the stdio path, and the test catches it.

**What the caller sees.**
- For a record from a previous connection, `gateway.tasks_get`,
  `tasks_result` and `tasks_cancel` fail with the "was reconnected"
  message. They do not send on the new connection. In practice this needs
  a record that outlived its connection, which the drop above prevents;
  the check is the backstop.
- A reply that arrives after its connection was replaced is not recorded.
  The caller still gets the record built from that reply; see Design
  decision 12 (rev 6). Rev 5 had widened `gateway.invoke`'s default redaction
  to any reply that carries a task id. Rev 6 reverts that to main's rule: a
  task was requested and the reply carries one
  (`test_invoke_redacts_by_default_only_when_a_task_was_requested`, mutant
  M65). Rev 5's M55 targeted the widened rule and is gone; the rule is now
  pinned by M60 and M65.

**Tests.**
- codex's falsifier, as written, with rev 5's required keywords added:
  `test_teardown_does_not_send_old_context_to_a_replacement_server`;
- the three bound sends refused: `get`, `result` and `cancel`;
- the teardown skipping an entry from a previous connection;
- the five record paths, each with its connection replaced in flight
  (`tools/call`, `tasks/list`, `tasks/get`, `tasks/result`, `tasks/cancel`):
  the reply is not recorded;
- the invoke redaction case;
- the two structural tests.

**Mutants M46–M56** cover:
- storing a replaced connection's reply;
- the funnel ignoring the binding;
- the teardown checking only the server, or sending unbound;
- `get_task_result`, `get_task` or `cancel_task` sending unbound;
- one shared connection id;
- an unstamped record;
- the invoke redaction;
- a replacement path that keeps the old records.
