### 12. A handler uses this reply's record, never a lookup by key after an await (rev 6, round-5 F001)

**The finding.** It is the inbound mirror of codex's round-4 finding. Three
handlers awaited the manager and then looked the task up by
`(server, task_id)`: `gateway.invoke`, `gateway.tasks_result` and
`gateway.tasks_list`.

Rev 5 rightly stopped storing a reply from a replaced connection. But if the
new connection already tracked a same-id task, the lookup returned *that*
task, possibly another tenant's, as the answer to the old connection's
reply. `gateway.invoke` then also suppressed the caller's own result, because
the result is `None` when a task is shown. Measured with `readback.py`
(*Verbatim bodies*): a call runs on A; B replaces A and tracks its own `t`
(`from-B`); A's reply names `t` (`from-A`).

| Tree | `gateway.invoke` task | `gateway.tasks_result` task / result | `gateway.tasks_list` |
|---|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `from-A` | `from-A` / `{'v': 'A'}` | `['from-A']` |
| rev 5 `efc9bb6` | **`from-B`** | **`from-B`** / `{'v': 'A'}` | **`['from-B']`** |
| rev 6 `bd2838b` | `from-A` | `from-A` / `{'v': 'A'}` | `['from-A']` |

**The rule.** A handler never re-derives task state by key after an await. It
uses the record pmcp built from *this* reply, on *this* connection.
- **`TaskReply(result, task)`** is a `NamedTuple`. `task` is the record
  `_record_task` built from the reply, stored or not: a reply from a
  replaced connection is built and returned but not stored. It is `None`
  when the reply carries no task pmcp records.
- **`ClientManager.call_tool_with_task`** and
  **`get_task_result_with_task`** return a `TaskReply`.
  `get_task_result_with_task` returns the record of the task asked for: its
  payload record, or the `tasks/get` fallback's, and `None` for a reply about
  a different task (mutant M61).
- **`call_tool` and `get_task_result` keep their signatures and return only
  the reply.** They are thin wrappers, so `cli.py` and every test double
  that implements or patches them keep working.
- **`get_task` and `cancel_task`** already returned the record they built;
  that is unchanged.
- **`list_tasks`** already returned dumps of the records it built.

The handlers now use them:
- `invoke` takes `reply.task`;
- `tasks_result` takes `reply.task`;
- `tasks_list` validates the listed dump. It no longer prefers a lookup.

**Why the record is returned even when it is not stored.** The panel
suggested "None if it wasn't stored". Returning the built record instead
keeps main's output exactly: the reply's own task, `from-A` above. It
carries nothing of the new connection, because it is built from the reply
alone. It also keeps `gateway.invoke`'s default redaction as main had it,
"this call produced a task record". That is true exactly when the call
requested a task and the reply carries one (the round-5 non-blocking scope
point; `test_invoke_redacts_by_default_only_when_a_task_was_requested`,
mutant M65). With `None`, the replaced case would need a second rule for
redaction.

**The guard, derived.** `test_no_task_state_is_looked_up_after_an_await_unless_listed`
finds every function in `src/pmcp` that reads task state after one of its
awaits. A read is any reference to an accessor, to `_tasks` or to an inline
reader, and a read and an await in the same loop count too. The test
compares the set whole with `READ_AFTER_AWAIT`, 15 entries, each with its
reason:
- five functions record this reply through the choke point, which is bound
  to its connection;
- six drop or clear records;
- the teardown re-reads by key after its binding check;
- three handlers only count.

`test_the_after_await_scan_sees_a_handler_lookup` pins the scan on the
round-5 shape. Mutant M62 adds a lookup after an await to `tasks_get`, and
the guard catches it.

**Sends, by any means (rev 6, round-5 non-blocking).**
`test_every_downstream_request_method_is_classified` collects every
`_send_request` call's method, whether positional or `method=` keyword, as
an expression. It compares them whole with `SEND_METHODS` (9). It also
requires that every non-docstring `"tasks/…"` string in `src/pmcp` is the
method of a bound send in `TASK_SENDS`. Mutant M63 sends `tasks/cancel`
through a variable, and the test catches it.

**Test doubles.** The handlers now call `call_tool_with_task` and
`get_task_result_with_task`. The client-manager doubles that drive them mix
in `tests/task_reply_double.py:TaskReplyDouble`: `test_tools`, `conftest`
and `test_integration`. The mixin delegates to the double's own `call_tool`
and `get_task_result`, so per-test patches of those still apply, and it
answers with the double's record of the task the reply names. The two
`MagicMock` managers in `test_lazy_start` set `call_tool_with_task`. That
is test-only churn: 5 lines in four modules, and a 37-line helper module.

**Tests.**
- the seat's falsifier, `test_invoke_never_returns_the_replacement_connections_record`,
  adapted to rev 5's required keywords;
- `test_tasks_result_never_pairs_the_reply_with_the_replacements_record`;
- `test_tasks_result_does_not_take_a_reply_about_another_task` (mutant M61);
- `test_a_stdio_connect_drops_the_names_records_before_anything_else`;
- `test_tasks_list_never_shows_the_replacements_record`;
- the redaction-scope test;
- the after-await guard and its fixture;
- the send-method guard;
- the widened `_clients`-write guard.

**Mutants M57–M65 and M64b** cover:
- each handler's lookup restored (M57, M58, M59);
- `call_tool_with_task` returning no record (M60);
- another task's record taken as this one's (M61);
- a new lookup after an await (M62);
- a variable send method (M63);
- `update()` registration, and a keep-records drop (M64, M64b);
- the redaction widened to every task-shaped result (M65).

### 13. One connection per request, captured at entry (rev 7, round-6 F001/F002)

**The two findings.**
- **F001.** `_record_task` read the registry and merged `created_at`,
  `tool_id` and `requestor_context` from the existing record *before* it
  checked whether the reply's connection was still the server's. When B
  replaced A and recorded its own `t`, A's late reply came back carrying B's
  values. Sanitising drops the context and `tool_id`, but `created_at`
  reached the caller.
- **F002.** `get_task_result_with_task` bound its `tasks/get` fallback to
  the record it read at entry. After an eviction that record is `None`, so
  the fallback resolved the server by name. If B had replaced A during
  `tasks/result`, A's context went to B, and A's result came back paired
  with B's task. That broke isolation both ways, on the documented recovery
  path for evicted tasks.

Measured with `isolation.py` (*Verbatim bodies*):

| Tree | F001: A's reply `(created_at, tool_id, context)` | F002: requests to B / output task |
|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `(222.0, 'tasks::B', {'tenant': 'B'})` | `['tasks/get']` / `from-B` |
| rev 6 `e4e9044` | `(222.0, 'tasks::B', {'tenant': 'B'})` | `['tasks/get']` / `from-B` |
| rev 7 `5456bb9` | `(111.0, None, None)` | `[]` / `None` (the result `{'value': 'from-A'}` is returned) |

Main shows the same: it has no notion of connections, so a replacement's
state mixes freely. Both findings are gaps in the isolation this plan
introduced.

**The rule, with the degree of freedom removed.** A request captures its
`ManagedClient` **once, at entry**, and every step uses that object:
- **Resolution, once.** `get_task`, `get_task_result_with_task` and
  `_send_task_cancel` each call `_task_client(server, bound_to=…)` exactly
  once, before their first await. `list_tasks` and the teardown resolve once
  per iteration, because each server or snapshot entry is its own request.
- **Fallbacks take the captured connection.** `_fetch_task(…, connection=)`
  no longer resolves anything. Both `get_task` and the `get_task_result`
  fallback pass it the connection they captured. The fallback runs only
  while `_owns(server, managed)`: the connection is still the server's. If
  it was replaced, there is nothing to continue on, and the result is
  returned with no task. It is never re-sent to the replacement.
- **Ownership before merge.** `_record_task(…, connection=)` first decides
  `owned`: the connection is the server's. If it is not, the record is built
  from the reply alone, with no registry read and no merge, and it is
  returned unstored. If it is owned, the merge still skips an existing
  record stamped with a *different* connection. That is a backstop, since a
  replacement drops the name's records first.
- **`_owns(server, connection)`** is an identity check against a captured
  connection. It is the only way a request may look at `_clients` after its
  first await.

**Guards.**
- `test_no_request_resolves_its_connection_again_after_an_await` derives the
  request functions from the AST. These are the async functions in the
  manager that send `tools/call` or a `tasks/*` method, call
  `_fetch_task`/`_send_task_cancel`, or resolve with `_task_client`. There
  are 8. After its first await, none may call `_task_client` or
  `_task_client_unavailable`, or touch `_clients`. The two per-iteration
  resolvers are listed, and must resolve only at the top of the iteration.
  The test also ties the generated test's operations to the derived public
  request functions.
- The binding table (`BINDINGS`) now lists every `_task_client` resolution
  (4) and every `_fetch_task(connection=…)` (2). In `TASK_SENDS`,
  `_fetch_task`'s send is "the caller's capture".

**The generated test.**
`test_no_task_op_sends_to_records_from_or_merges_a_replacement` covers 7
reply paths × {tracked, evicted}, 14 cases:
- `tools/call`, `tasks/list` and `tasks/get`;
- the `tasks/result` payload and the `tasks/result` → `tasks/get` fallback;
- `tasks/cancel` and the teardown cancel.

In each case, B replaces A during the operation's first request and records
its own `t` with `from-B`, `created_at` 222, `tasks::B` and tenant B. The
test asserts three things:
- nothing is sent to B;
- B's record is still the registry's, unchanged;
- nothing the operation returns carries any of B's four values.

On rev 6's manager, 12 of the 14 fail, measured. The two that pass are the
tracked fallback (rev 6 refused it by binding) and the teardown (rev 5's
binding check).

**Falsifiers.** Both of codex's are in the module as written:
`test_late_reply_does_not_merge_replacement_state` and
`test_evicted_result_fallback_never_sends_to_replacement`. Both fail on
rev 6.

**Each merge layer is pinned on its own.**
- `test_a_reply_on_a_connection_that_is_not_the_servers_reads_nothing`
  covers ownership first. A non-owned reply ignores even an *unbound*
  registry record, which the second layer would not catch.
- `test_a_leftover_record_from_another_connection_is_never_merged` covers the
  second layer. An owned reply ignores a record stamped with another
  connection.

The first rev 7 mutation run showed why both are needed: M66 (merge before
the check) survived, because the second layer covered it, and M67 survived
because nothing exercised the second layer. M66b, which is rev 6's code
exactly (both layers gone), was killed by codex's falsifier. With the two
tests added, M66, M66b and M67 are each killed.

**Mutants** (M66, M66b, M67–M71), plus M29, M46, M50 and M51 re-pointed at
the new code:
- the merge before the ownership check (M66), and rev 6's merge exactly
  (M66b);
- a merge with another connection's record (M67);
- an unbound fallback (M68);
- the fallback on a replaced connection (M69);
- `_fetch_task` resolving by name again (M70);
- a request touching `_clients` after its await (M71).

### 14. The newest context pmcp holds wins (rev 8, round-7 codex F001)

*Superseded in part by Design decision 15 (rev 9). The rule "supplied, else
the existing record's" stands. The captured `fallback_context` described
here is removed, because pinning keeps the existing record.*

**The finding.**
- Rev 7's `_record_reply` passed the context it captured before its await as
  `_record_task`'s `requestor_context`, which wins over the existing record.
- The codex seat's sequence:
  1. `invoke` records `t` with `old`;
  2. `tasks/get` captures `old` and awaits;
  3. a second `invoke` on the same connection records `t` with `new`;
  4. the late `tasks/get` reply writes `old` back;
  5. `tasks_cancel` sends `old`.
- Main kept `new`, because its `get_task` recorded with no context and the
  merge kept the existing one.
- Connection ownership does not help, because both requests use the same
  connection.

**The rule, at the choke point.** There are two kinds of context, and
`_record_task` requires both as keywords with no default:
- `requestor_context` is supplied by a caller *with this request*. Only
  `call_tool_with_task` passes one: `gateway.invoke`'s
  `task.requestor_context`. It wins, as on main, because the caller has just
  said so.
- `fallback_context` was *held* by pmcp and captured before an await. Every
  reply passes one through `_record_reply`. It never overrides. An existing
  same-connection record's context is kept, and the captured copy fills in
  only when there is none. That is exactly what an eviction during the
  await leaves behind, which is rev 4's purpose.

Precedence: **supplied, else the existing same-connection record's, else
the captured fallback.** Rev 7 already restricts the merge to the same
connection.

**Every caller re-checked.** `CONTEXT_FLOW` now pins both parameters at each
`_record_task` call, 13 entries in all:
- `call_tool_with_task`: supplied = the caller's context; fallback = `None`.
- `list_tasks`: `None` and `None`. A tracked task keeps its own context.
- `_record_reply`: supplied = `None`; fallback = the held context, unless
  the reply names another task.

The chains that reach `_record_reply` are unchanged:
- `_fetch_task`, `get_task_result_with_task` and `_send_task_cancel`, with
  the context they held;
- `cancel_task`, with the record's;
- the teardown, with current-else-snapshot.

**What changed for the earlier rounds.** Rev 4 and rev 5 made the held
context survive an eviction. It still does: with no record, the fallback is
used (`…keeps_the_held_context`, M74). What it can no longer do is overwrite
a newer context.

**Measured with `context_race.py`** (*Verbatim bodies*). The older request is
`tasks/get`, `tasks/result` or `tasks/cancel`; a newer `invoke` lands during
its await; this runs with and without an eviction. Then the next cancel:

| Tree | Context sent by the next cancel (all six cases) |
|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `{'correlation': 'new'}` |
| rev 7 `879b2ba` | `{'correlation': 'old'}` |
| rev 8 | `{'correlation': 'new'}` |

**Tests.**
- codex's falsifier, as written: `test_late_status_reply_preserves_newer_context`.
- `test_the_newest_context_wins_on_one_connection`, a generated test over 5
  held-context paths × 2 orders × 2 eviction states, 20 cases:
  - the paths are `tasks/get`, `tasks/result`, the `tasks/result` →
    `tasks/get` fallback, `tasks/cancel` and the teardown cancel;
  - the orders are "the newer call lands during the await" and "after the
    reply";
  - the eviction states are none, and `t` evicted during the await.

  In every case pmcp ends up holding `NEW`, and the next cancel sends it. On
  rev 7's manager, the falsifier and the five "newer call lands first,
  no eviction" cases fail, measured. The eviction cases cannot run there,
  because rev 7 has no `fallback_context` keyword; `context_race.py` is
  their semantic counterpart.

**Mutants:**
- M72: rev 7's rule, where the reply's captured context is supplied;
- M73: the fallback beats the existing record;
- M74: the fallback is never used.

**The resolution guard follows helpers (rev 8, round-7 claude note).** The
seat showed that the guard against resolving by name after an await missed a
plain helper doing the lookup. The behavioural 14-case test still caught it,
with 3 cases failing. `_client_resolvers` now closes the resolver set over
every plain helper that touches `_clients` or calls a resolver, to a
fixpoint, as the read scan does. A plain helper is a `def`, or an
`async def` with no await. `_owns` is the one named exception: it compares a
captured connection and hands no client back.
`test_the_resolution_guard_follows_helpers_but_not_owns` covers four shapes:
- `.get`;
- `[...]`;
- a helper of a helper;
- an await-free async helper.

Each is caught, and the `_owns` control is not.
- Mutant M75 is the seat's exact probe, a `_current_client` helper used by
  the fallback. It is killed in both passes by codex's round-6 F002
  falsifier. Run against the guard alone (`-k
  no_request_resolves_its_connection_again`), the guard kills it too.
- Mutant M76 makes the guard non-transitive, and the fixtures kill it.

### 15. A record with a request in flight cannot be evicted (rev 9, round-8 F001)

*Rev 11 narrows the teardown's part (Design decision 17): a teardown pins
only the entry whose `tasks/cancel` is in flight, and holds the rest of what
it owes in a custody. The rule for `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and
`tasks/cancel` is unchanged.*

**Why rev 9 changes approach.** Rounds 1, 3, 4, 7 and 8 each found one more
ordering of "the cap evicts a record while a request that read it is in
flight", and each revision added a rule for that ordering:
- skip it;
- cancel it by its snapshot;
- write the held context back;
- prefer the supplied context;
- a captured fallback.

Round 8's ordering is new call, then eviction, then the late reply, and it
defeated the fallback. Rev 9 removes the degree of freedom instead: **such a
record is pinned, and a pinned record is never evicted.**

**Pinning.**
- `TaskRegistry.pin(key)` and `unpin(key)` keep a refcount per
  `(server, task_id)`, so concurrent requests on one key nest. A key need not
  hold a record yet. An `unpin` without its `pin` raises `KeyError`: a leak
  or a double release is a bug, not a no-op.
- `ClientManager._pinned(keys)` is a context manager, and the only place
  `pin` and `unpin` are called: pin, `try: yield`, `finally: unpin`. It is
  used only as a `with` item, so a request releases its pins however it ends.
- `@_pins_task` decorates `get_task`, `get_task_result_with_task` and
  `cancel_task`. It pins `(server_name, task_id)` before the body reads the
  record. The teardown helper pins every snapshot entry for the whole loop.
- `test_pins_are_taken_and_released_only_by_the_one_context_manager`
  enforces three things:
  - `pin`/`unpin` appear only in `_pinned`;
  - `_pinned` appears only as a `with` item;
  - every request function that reads a record before its await is pinned.

  It derives that last set, which is exactly `get_task`,
  `get_task_result_with_task`, `cancel_task` and
  `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown`.

**Eviction skips pins.** `_candidate` never returns a pinned key. If a
server's only remaining records are pinned (plus the one just stored), the
per-server loop stops, and the server is over its cap. The total loop does
the same across servers. When the last pin on a key is released, `unpin`
re-applies both caps to that server, so the slack is given back at once.

**The bound, at every moment** (*rev 9's form, superseded by Design
decision 16: it depends on `pinned`, which round 9 showed can grow with
overlapping teardowns*):

    len(server s) <= max(per_server, pinned records of s + 1)
    len(registry) <= max(total, pinned records + 1)

Pinned records are at most one per in-flight `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` or
`tasks/cancel`, plus each in-flight teardown's snapshot. The snapshot is the
server's active records, which are already within this bound. So the slack
is bounded by in-flight concurrency, and returns to zero when the requests
end. The generated test wraps `put` and `unpin` and checks the bound after
every call, on all 90 cases.

**Simplified, because pinning makes these unreachable.**
- **`fallback_context` (rev 8) is removed.** A request that captured a
  context pinned its record, so the record is still there when the reply
  lands. A reply records with `requestor_context=None`, and the existing
  record's context, the newest pmcp holds, is kept. The only way a pinned
  record goes is a disconnect, and that replaces or removes the connection,
  so the reply is not stored anyway (rev 5 and rev 7). Removing it changed
  no outcome: every rev 4, 5 and 8 context test passes.
- **The `stored_context` chain (rev 5) and `_record_reply` are removed.**
  `_fetch_task` and `_send_task_cancel` record their reply directly, with
  `requestor_context=None`. `CONTEXT_FLOW` is now 5 entries, one per
  `_record_task` call.
- **The teardown's "record disappeared" branch is now an invariant.** After
  its binding check passes, the connection is the snapshot's, so no
  disconnect dropped the pinned record. The record is asserted present, and
  its own context is sent. The snapshot's copy is no longer needed (mutant
  M27 sends it and is killed).
- **Tests that asserted eviction now assert survival.** Since rev 2, tests
  had pushed "x" and "y" during an await and asserted that the task was
  evicted. They now assert that the pinned task survived, and their outcome
  assertions (the context sent, the cancel counted) are unchanged.

**What is unchanged.** An eviction *before* a request starts can still lose
a context (the residual since rev 3). The generated test treats it
explicitly: when NEW is evicted while no request pins `t`, the test asserts
only that OLD never comes back.

**Measured with `context_race.py`**, for each older request (`tasks/get`,
`tasks/result`, `tasks/cancel`) and each eviction order, the context sent
by the next cancel:

| Tree | No eviction | Evicted before the newer call (round 7) | Evicted after the newer call (round 8) |
|---|---|---|---|
| main `31c1357` | `new` | `new` | `new` |
| rev 8 `212e9d6` | `new` | `new` | **`old`** |
| rev 9 | `new` | `new` | `new` |

**Tests.**
- The claude seat's falsifier, as written:
  `test_a_newer_context_evicted_mid_await_is_not_replaced_by_the_older`.
- The codex seat's, as written:
  `test_newer_held_context_survives_an_older_reply_after_eviction`.
  It has two polls and a listing in between.
- Both fail on rev 8's manager, measured.
- `test_the_teardown_sends_each_records_current_context`: while `a`'s cancel
  awaits, a newer call supplies NEW for `b`, and `b`'s cancel carries NEW.
- `test_a_pinned_record_keeps_the_newest_context_in_every_order`, 90 cases.
  It crosses:
  - the 5 held-context paths;
  - the newer call before, during or after the await;
  - the flood of other tasks before the newer call, after it, or after
    everything;
  - the server's other records pinned at the cap, or not.

  It asserts that:
  - `t` is never evicted while pinned;
  - the bound holds at every `put`/`unpin`;
  - every pin is released, and the slack is given back;
  - the next cancel sends NEW, unless NEW was evicted while nothing pinned
    `t`, and even then OLD never returns.
- `test_every_pin_is_released_however_the_request_ends` covers 4 pinning
  operations × {reply, exception, cancellation, timeout}, 16 cases. The pin
  is asserted while the request awaits, and the count is zero after it.
- An autouse fixture, `_no_pin_leaks`, wraps every `TaskRegistry` built
  during each test in the module, and fails the test if any pin is left.
- Registry tests: the slack is given back on unpin, pins nest, an
  unbalanced unpin raises, and the total cap skips pins.

**Mutants M77–M85:**
- eviction ignores pins;
- a pin is released only on success;
- releasing the last pin keeps the slack;
- each of `get_task`, `get_task_result_with_task`, `cancel_task` and the
  teardown unpinned;
- pins do not nest;
- an unbalanced unpin is silently ignored.

M27 and M72 are re-pointed: the teardown sends the snapshot's context, and a
reply supplies its captured context. M27 is killed by
`test_the_teardown_sends_each_records_current_context`. Rev 5–8's context
mutants M24, M38, M42a–i, M73 and M74 target code rev 9 removed, and are
retired.

M29 is retired too. It made the `tasks/result` fallback re-read the record
for the context it *sends*. Under pinning, the record is the same one the
request read at entry, so a re-read can differ only by a newer context a
concurrent `invoke` supplied. Sending that newer context is at least as
correct, so "read once" is no longer a rule to pin. The first rev 9 run
showed this: M29 survived.

The first rev 9 mutation run also had a concurrency fault. After an API
interruption, its behavioural pass was still running when I resumed and
edited the tree. I stopped editing, confirmed that nothing was running,
restored `src/` from the spike commit (checked with `git hash-object`),
committed only the two new tests, and re-ran the whole chain once, alone.
Only that run is reported.

### 16. Forced teardowns are serialised per server; the bound depends only on caps and requests (rev 10, round-9 codex F001)

*Superseded by Design decision 17 (rev 11): the lock is removed, and a
teardown no longer pins its snapshot. Kept as the record of round 9.*

**The finding.** Rev 9 pinned a teardown's whole snapshot. Two forced
disconnects of one server could overlap in a handoff:
1. the second cancels the first's in-flight `tasks/cancel`
   (`cancel_pending_requests`);
2. it then pins its own snapshot synchronously, before the first resumes
   and releases;
3. that snapshot includes the record that only the first teardown's pins had
   kept past the cap.

One more record is retained per handoff. Codex measured 100 → 1,101 after
1,001 handoffs, with at most two teardowns overlapping. Rev 9's inequality
`records <= max(cap, pinned + 1)` stayed true only because `pinned` grew,
so it was not a bound in terms of concurrency.

**Options weighed.**
- **(a) A teardown pins nothing.** The disconnect drops the server's records
  anyway. But `cancel_active_tasks` (a forced refresh) does not disconnect
  an unchanged server, and an unpinned snapshot reopens the eviction
  orderings of rounds 1, 3, 4, 7 and 8 for the teardown: cancel-by-snapshot
  and which context to send. That brings back the rules rev 9 removed.
- **(c) Pin only the records within the cap.** The overflow entries would
  need the unpinned rules again. It is two mechanisms where one suffices.
- **(b) Serialise forced teardowns per server. Chosen.** It removes the
  degree of freedom, two snapshots of one server pinned at once, and keeps
  rev 9's single rule ("a record a request is working on stays") with no
  exceptions.

**The mechanism.**
- `ClientManager._teardown_locks` holds one `asyncio.Lock` per server name.
- `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown(server_names)` now takes server names, not a
  snapshot. For each server in turn, one lock at a time, so nothing can
  deadlock, it does three things under the lock:
  1. reads the server's active records, so the snapshot is read *inside*
     the lock;
  2. pins them;
  3. cancels them.
- `disconnect_server` and `cancel_active_tasks` call it with server names.
- A second forced teardown of the server first cancels the in-flight
  requests, as before. That ends the first teardown, which releases its pins
  in `finally`, and the caps are re-applied. The second then takes the lock
  and reads a fresh snapshot.

**The bound, derived.** Every `put` and `unpin` leaves
`records(s) <= max(per_server, pinned(s) + 1)`, and
`records <= max(total, pinned + 1)`. With serialisation:
- `pinned(s) <= R + S(s)`, where S(s) is the size of the one teardown
  snapshot of s in flight;
- S(s) is read when no teardown of s holds pins, so
  `S(s) <= max(per_server, R + 1)`.

Therefore:

    records(s) <= max(per_server, R + max(per_server, R + 1) + 1)
    records    <= max(total, R + T * max(per_server, R + 1) + 1)

with R the in-flight `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and `tasks/cancel` requests
(one pin each), and T the servers with a teardown in flight. Neither
depends on how many records happen to be pinned, or on history. At the
defaults, with no other request in flight, a server holds at most 101
records. Past the total, a smaller server may give up a record while larger
ones are held only by pins (round-9 N2). That is stated next to the bound in
`task_registry.py`.

**Tests.**
- `test_overlapping_forced_disconnects_retain_a_bounded_number_of_records`
  is codex's handoff loop, with real `disconnect_server` calls and real
  `PendingRequest`s that a later disconnect cancels. It runs 1,001
  handoffs at the default caps, with one new task per handoff, and asserts
  a peak of at most 101. On rev 9's manager it fails at `(102, 101)`,
  measured.
- `test_retention_is_bounded_by_caps_and_requests_in_flight` covers
  {2, 3} overlapping teardowns per round × {0, 1, 3} in-flight
  get/result/cancel requests × {forced disconnect, `cancel_active_tasks`},
  over 40 rounds with floods. The server and total counts are checked
  against the formula after every `put` and `unpin`, and every pin is
  released at the end. 6 of the 12 cases fail on rev 9's manager.
- `test_teardowns_of_one_server_are_serialised_under_its_lock` is
  structural: the snapshot read and the pin happen only inside the
  `async with` on the server's teardown lock, and the helper takes server
  names.
- `test_a_failing_release_does_not_leak_the_other_pins` (N3) and
  `test_a_teardown_skips_an_entry_whose_record_is_unexpectedly_gone` (N1).

**Mutants:**
- M86: the teardowns are not serialised;
- M87: one failing release leaks the rest;
- M88: the invariant is an `assert` again.

M25, M26, M28, M28b, M49 and M78 are re-pointed at the new code.

### 17. A teardown owes what was tracked at its request, and holds it outside the caps (rev 11, round-10 codex F001)

*Rev 12 amends this (Design decision 18): an owed task in a custody is still
a tracked task for every reader, so a later teardown inherits it, and the
custody bound below -- "a bound in R at its request" -- is replaced by the
obligation bound. The registry bound stands.*

**The finding, two parts.**
1. *A forced refresh missed a task.* Rev 10's helper took server names and
   read each server's snapshot under that server's lock, when its turn came.
   `cancel_active_tasks()` over alpha and beta: while alpha's `tasks/cancel`
   awaited, a `tasks/list` on beta recorded 100 tasks and evicted `beta::b`.
   Beta's snapshot, read afterwards, held the 100 new tasks and not `b`, so
   `b` kept running on the downstream. Rev 9 had cancelled it.
2. *The bound failed when requests finished.* 1,100 polls in flight pin
   1,100 records; a teardown snapshots and pins them; the polls finish. Rev
   10 retained 1,100 records with R = 0 against a stated 101. The proof read
   the snapshot's size with R at the snapshot, then stated it with R now;
   the generated test fixed R at its peak, so it checked neither.

Both reproduced natively on the rev 10 spike, with codex's falsifier as
written: `{'beta_cancelled': False, 'result': (101, [])}` and
`{'R': 0, 'T': 1, 'retained': 1100, 'server_bound': 101}`.

**The class.** The obligation is "every record tracked for these servers
when the teardown was requested". Anything that reads it later, or lets
eviction change it, is the part-1 bug in another ordering. And as long as
the obligation is held by *pins*, it counts against the caps: in the round-9
handoff loop each aborted teardown's successor owes, and pins, one record
more than its predecessor, so retention grows with history. Rev 10 bought
the bound by reading late; that is part 1. The two requirements are only
compatible if what a teardown owes is held outside the registry's count.

**Options weighed.**
- *Pin the whole snapshot at request time, keep the lock.* Fixes part 1 but
  brings back round 9's growth: the snapshot must include the overflow the
  previous teardown's pins kept.
- *Cap what a teardown may pin.* The records past the cap would need the
  unpinned rules again (cancel by snapshot, which context): two mechanisms.
- **Chosen: a custody.** The teardown fixes the obligation at once, as data,
  and the registry keeps evicting as if no teardown ran; an evicted owed
  record moves into the teardown's custody instead of disappearing.

**The mechanism.**
- `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown(server_names)`: its first statement reads
  every active record of every named server (`sorted(set(server_names))`),
  before any await. `disconnect_server` and `cancel_active_tasks` call it
  with no await before the call, so "when it was requested" is exact.
- `with self._custody(snapshot) as owed:` registers a `TaskCustody` with the
  registry (`watch`) for the whole teardown and releases it in `finally`
  (`unwatch`). `_custody` is the only caller of either, and only as a `with`
  item; the autouse leak guard fails any test that leaves one registered.
- `TaskCustody` holds, per owed key, the latest record pmcp held: the
  snapshot's, replaced by the registry's record when `_evict` gives that up
  (`receive`). It never gains a key it does not owe, so it never grows.
- `_record_task` merges with `self._tasks.get(key) or self._tasks.held(key)`:
  when the registry no longer holds the key, the custody's record for it is
  the existing record. So a listing or reply that re-tracks an evicted owed
  task keeps its context, `created_at` and `tool_id`, exactly as if it had
  never been evicted. The binding check that follows still refuses a record
  from another connection. Every custody that owes a key receives every
  eviction of it, so while the registry does not hold the key they all hold
  the same, latest record, and `held` returns the first it finds (a
  disconnect drops records without an eviction, but then the connection is
  gone and nothing is merged).
- Per entry: the snapshot's connection is checked first (rev 5); then the
  entry is pinned for its own request (`with self._pinned([key])`), and the
  latest record is the registry's, else the custody's. Finished: counted,
  nothing sent. Otherwise `tasks/cancel` with that record's context. The
  pin is the same one `cancel_task` takes: a teardown has at most one
  request in flight, and that request's record cannot be evicted under it.
- `_teardown_locks` is removed. Teardowns no longer pin what they owe, so
  overlapping teardowns cannot stack retention, and there is nothing left to
  serialise.

**Round-10 claude notes.**
- **N1** (`_teardown_locks` never pruned): the dict no longer exists.
- **N2** (a forced disconnect could wait behind a forced refresh's whole
  multi-server teardown): there is no lock to wait on. A forced disconnect
  and a forced refresh of the same server run concurrently, as on main and
  rev 9. Each cancels what it owes; a task the other's cancel has already
  finished is counted with nothing sent, and otherwise both send
  `tasks/cancel`, which a downstream answers for a finished task as it would
  any repeated cancel.

**The bounds, derived, at every instant.**
- *Registry.* Eviction skips only pinned keys and the record `put` just
  stored, and releasing the last pin on a key re-applies the caps at once.
  So after every `put` and `unpin`, `records(s) <= max(per_server, P(s) + 1)`
  and `records <= max(total, P + 1)`, with P the pinned keys. Pins are taken
  only by in-flight `tasks/get`, `tasks/result` and `tasks/cancel` requests,
  one each, a teardown's current cancel included. So with R the requests in
  flight *now*:

      records of one server <= max(per_server, R(s) + 1)
      all records           <= max(total, R + 1)

  When a request finishes, its unpin re-applies the caps in the same step,
  so the bound shrinks with R. Custodies are not in the registry and do not
  count.
- *Custodies.* A custody is exactly its request-time snapshot: active
  records of its servers, then at most `max(per_server, R_req(s) + 1)` per
  server and `max(total, R_req + 1)` in all, with R_req the requests in
  flight at its request. `receive` replaces an entry and never adds one, so
  that size is fixed until the teardown ends. This is a bound in the peak
  concurrency while the teardown runs, and it is documented as such in
  `task_registry.py`; it is memory a running teardown holds, released when it
  ends, never registry retention.

Codex's two orderings: part 1 cancels `beta::b` from the custody, with its
context; part 2 retains 100 records once the polls finish (R = 0), while the
teardown's custody holds its 1,100-entry snapshot until it is cancelled.

**What the tests check (the exact claims above).**
- `_LiveBounds` wraps `put`, `unpin` and `watch`. After each, it reads R
  live (the sum of the pin counts) and checks both registry bounds; checks
  that R never exceeds the requests the test has in flight; checks a new
  custody's per-server size against `max(per_server, R(s) + 1)` at that
  instant; and checks that no custody ever changes size. Codex's handoff
  loop, the 12 overlap variants and the interleaving test all use it.
- `test_teardown_snapshot_acceptance`: codex's falsifier, as written.
- `test_every_interleaving_cancels_what_was_owed_within_the_live_bound`:
  all 180 orderings of six events, for a forced refresh of alpha and beta
  and a forced disconnect of beta (360 runs): T, the teardown; P and p, a
  poll on `beta::b1` starting and later answered (R grows, then shrinks
  while the custody is held: part 2's shape); F, a flood of two tasks on
  beta (part 1's cross-server eviction while alpha's cancel waits); N, a
  newer context for `beta::b2`; S, one cancel answered. Caps 2 per server, 3
  in total. An independent model, kept by the test and not read from the
  custody, tracks the newest context pmcp holds for each task (a supplied
  context sets it; an eviction loses it only if no teardown in flight owed
  the task). For every ordering: the live bounds after every operation;
  every task owed at the request gets a `tasks/cancel`; every cancel carries
  the model's context; the refresh returns `(len(owed), [])`; no pin or
  custody is left.
- `test_a_pinned_record_keeps_the_newest_context_in_every_order`, teardown
  cases (18): the teardown owes `t` from its request, pins it only for its
  own cancel, and sends the newest context pmcp held, the custody's
  included; a `t` evicted while owed is re-tracked with it.
- Unit tests: a custody keeps an evicted owed record and never grows; the
  context survives evicted-NEW, re-tracked context-less, evicted again;
  every custody owing a key holds its latest record; one pin at a time; the
  custody is released on exception and cancellation; an unbalanced
  `unwatch` raises.
- `test_a_teardown_fixes_what_it_owes_before_its_first_await` (structural):
  the snapshot is the helper's first statement and reads every server; no
  await precedes the helper's call in `disconnect_server` or
  `cancel_active_tasks`; `_custody` is used once, as a `with` item; no
  `_teardown_locks`.
- `test_a_teardown_cancels_an_entry_gone_from_the_registry_from_its_custody`
  replaces round-9 N1's skip: an owed record the registry no longer returns
  is cancelled from the custody, with its context.

**Red on rev 10** (rev 10's `manager.py` and `task_registry.py` in the rev
11 tree, with a one-line `class TaskCustody` stub so the module imports): 24
of the rev 11 tests fail. These include codex's falsifier (both parts),
both interleaving runs, the handoff loop and all 12 overlap variants (each
on `R <= in_flight`: rev 10's teardown pins more keys than it has requests
in flight), the structural guard and the custody tests.

**Mutants M89–M100**, and M27, M28, M28b, M49, M66, M66b and M83 re-pointed
at the new code; M86 (the lock) and M88 (the N1 `assert`) retired with the
code they targeted. See the *Mutation table*.

### 18. An owed task is a tracked task, for every reader (rev 12, round-11 F001)

**The finding.** Rev 11 moved an evicted owed record into the owing
teardown's custody, but `get_active_tasks` read only the registry. Four
readers stopped seeing the task at once: the forced-disconnect snapshot, the
non-forced disconnect refusal, the refresh gate (`active_tasks_seen`) and
`mcp_tasks_remaining`. The seat's ordering: a forced refresh owes
`alpha::a1` and `beta::b1`; while `a1`'s cancel waits, two new beta tasks
evict `b1` into the refresh's custody; another caller force-disconnects
beta, owes only `f0` and `f1`, cancels them and removes the connection; the
refresh reaches `b1`, finds beta gone, and skips it. Nobody sends
`tasks/cancel` for `b1`; the refresh returns `(1, [])`. A non-forced
disconnect in the same window returns `(True, 0, None)`. Rev 9 passed this
(it pinned `b1`), and main never evicted an unfinished task.

**The class.** Rev 11 created a second place a task can be active, and
every reader consulted the first only. Fixing the four readers one by one
would leave the next one; the fix is one source.

**The mechanism.**
- `TaskRegistry.owed_records(server=None)`: the records live custodies hold
  that the registry does not, once per key. Every custody owing a key
  received every eviction of it, so any one's record is the latest (the
  argument `held` already makes).
- `ClientManager.get_tracked_tasks(server=None)`, the one source: the
  registry's records, plus `owed_records` whose `_connection_id` is the
  server's current connection (`_on_current_connection`; an unbound record
  belongs to whichever is current). A removed or replaced connection's task
  is not this server's state (rev 5): without the filter a refresh's own
  custody would count tasks of a server it had just disconnected, and an old
  connection's task would refuse a disconnect of its replacement.
- `get_active_tasks` is built on it, so every reader the grep finds sees an
  owed task: `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown`'s snapshot, `disconnect_server`'s
  refusal and `force` path, `cancel_active_tasks`'s server list,
  `GatewayTools.refresh`'s gate and `mcp_tasks_remaining`, and the
  disconnect and restart handlers' before/after counts.
- **The request readers (codex's round-11 F001).** `get_task`,
  `get_task_result_with_task`, `cancel_task` and the teardown's per-entry
  cancel each read one key with `get_task_record`, after pinning it. In rev
  11 a key held only in a custody read as absent: the request sent no
  context, and if the owning teardown was then cancelled, the custody -- the
  only copy -- was released, so the reply and the next cancel kept `None`.
  Rev 12: `_pinned`, the only place a pin is taken, puts such a record back
  into the registry (`TaskRegistry.restore`) after taking the pin, so the
  caps the restore re-applies cannot evict it, and only when the record
  belongs to the server's current connection. From then until the request
  ends the record is in the registry, whatever the custody does. So:
  - a record a request is working on never lives only in a custody, and
    releasing a custody can drop only what nothing pins and no other
    running teardown owes (another owner already holds it: every custody
    owing a key received every eviction of it);
  - `get_task_record` stays the registry's, read only under such a pin
    (structurally pinned: its only callers are those four), so Design
    decision 5's contract for a task evicted while nothing owes it is
    unchanged;
  - the teardown's rev 11 fallback to its custody's record is removed: its
    per-entry pin restores the record, and the "no record" branch is again
    an explicit, unreachable skip (round-9 N1).
  - the restore re-records the task as the server's newest (it takes a new
    `_recorded_order`): it is the task a request is working on.
- `test_every_reader_of_task_state_goes_through_get_tracked_tasks` pins it:
  the registry is read only by `get_tracked_tasks`, `get_task_record` and
  `_record_task`'s merge, and `_custodies` only inside `TaskRegistry`.

**A record another teardown already owes.** Options: wait for the owner, or
cancel it now. Waiting brings back rev 10's lock and its N2 (a disconnect
waiting on another server's cancel), and the owner may be the one that
finds the connection gone. **Chosen: cancel it now.** The second teardown's
snapshot includes it, so it is in the second custody too, and it is
cancelled like any snapshot entry. If the owner reaches it later, it finds
it finished (the reply was recorded, counted, nothing sent) or its
connection gone (skipped: the second teardown has sent its cancel). Two
cancels for one task are possible, as under N2.

**The trilemma, and which side rev 12 takes.** These three cannot all hold
when forced teardowns chain without any cancel being answered:
1. never drop a task a teardown owes;
2. hold what teardowns owe in memory bounded by live concurrency;
3. a forced disconnect aborts the in-flight requests before it, its
   predecessor's `tasks/cancel` included.

Rev 9 kept 1 by growing the registry (round 9). Rev 10 kept 2 by dropping
(round 10). Rev 11 seemed to keep both only because custodies did not see
each other (round 11).

*Weighed and rejected: owed records stay in the registry, exempt from
eviction, under a separate "owed" budget* (the coordinator's suggestion for
round 12). That is rev 9's mechanism. It removes the second store, but it
makes every owed record registry retention, so codex's round-10 falsifier,
which is in the module as written and must keep passing, fails again: with
1,100 finished polls and one teardown it retains 1,100 records against a
`server_bound` of 101 at R = 0. The obligation therefore has to live outside
the registry's count, and rev 12 keeps the custody and closes the
visibility gap instead: one source for every reader of active tasks, and a
pin that restores a custody's record for the request that takes it. Rev 12 keeps 1 and 3, keeps the registry bound in
live requests exactly as rev 11 stated it, and bounds custodies by
obligations instead. Weighed and deferred: discharging an obligation when its
cancel is *sent* (all of a teardown's cancels in one tick) would make the
custody bound history-free, but it changes the one-at-a-time order and the
"a downstream error propagates" semantics the module pins (`sent == ['a',
'b']`); it is not adopted this round.

**The bounds.**
- *Registry* (unchanged from rev 11; N1's wording): with R(s) the requests
  on s in flight now and R their sum, `records(s) <= max(per_server, R(s) +
  1)` and `records <= max(total, R + 1)`, after every `put` and `unpin`.
- *Custodies*: a custody is exactly its request-time snapshot, never larger:
  what the registry held for its servers then (at most
  `max(per_server, R(s) + 1)` per server) plus the tasks pmcp then tracked
  only through custodies. Those -- `get_tracked_tasks(s)` minus the
  registry -- are at most the owed records the registry has evicted since
  the current unbroken chain of overlapping teardowns of s began. Each is a
  distinct downstream task some teardown in the chain was asked to cancel
  and none has had answered. The quantity is history within the chain, not
  live concurrency; it is zero once no teardown of s runs, and the memory is
  released when the chain ends. Main kept every such task in the registry,
  uncapped and forever.
- *Measured, codex's handoff loop* (1,001 forced disconnects, each aborting
  the last, one new task per handoff, no cancel ever answered): the registry
  peaks at 101; the newest custody owes 100 + 1,000 tasks. The test asserts
  both numbers exactly.

**What the tests check.**
- `_LiveBounds` gains the obligation bound: after every `put`, `unpin` and
  `watch`, the tasks of s tracked only through custodies never exceed the
  owed evictions of s counted since its chain began (it counts them by
  wrapping `_evict`, and resets when no custody owes anything of s); a new
  custody's per-server size is at most `max(per_server, R(s) + 1)` plus
  that quantity at its request. The registry bound and "a custody never
  grows" are unchanged.
- `test_owed_task_in_custody_is_cancelled_despite_a_concurrent_disconnect`:
  the seat's falsifier, as written.
- `test_a_non_forced_disconnect_refuses_on_a_task_a_teardown_owes`: the
  seat's variant; the refresh then cancels `b1` with its context.
- `test_overlapping_teardowns_and_disconnects_never_drop_an_owed_task`:
  three alphabets, each enumerated whole (360 + 180 + 180 orderings):
  {forced refresh of both servers, forced disconnect of beta, non-forced
  disconnect of beta, two floods of beta, one answered cancel}, {two forced
  refreshes, forced disconnect, flood, two answered cancels} and {two forced
  disconnects, non-forced disconnect, two floods, one answered cancel}. The
  test keeps its own model -- never read from the registry or the custodies
  -- of the tasks running downstream, what each teardown owed at its
  request, and the newest context. For every ordering: the live and
  obligation bounds after every operation; every cancel carries the model's
  context; a non-forced disconnect never succeeds while the model has a beta
  task running; every task a teardown owed gets a `tasks/cancel`, from it or
  from another teardown that owed it too; no pin or custody is left.
- `test_an_owed_record_counts_only_on_its_servers_current_connection`,
  `test_owed_records_are_only_what_the_registry_gave_up_once_each`, and the
  structural reader guard.
- `test_custody_context_survives_a_concurrent_poll`: codex's falsifier, as
  written.
- `test_a_pin_restores_a_custodys_record_and_keeps_it_through_the_caps` (the
  restore runs after the pin: with every other record pinned, a restore
  before it would be evicted straight back) and
  `test_a_pin_never_restores_another_connections_record`.
- `test_an_owed_entry_the_cap_evicted_is_restored_by_its_pin_and_cancelled`
  and the round-9 N1 skip test, restored.
- The overlap harness gains four alphabets: an abort of the newest running
  teardown, `tasks/get` and `tasks/result` polls on an owed task, and a
  listing that re-tracks it. Its model keeps `custody` itself: a key the cap
  evicted while the model says a running teardown owed it; a pin or
  re-record takes it out; when no running teardown owes it and nothing pins
  it, it is lost. It asserts the context of every `tasks/get`,
  `tasks/result` and `tasks/cancel`, and at the end that every task it
  tracks is tracked with its context. An aborted teardown's own obligation
  ends with it (its caller sees the cancellation); every other teardown's is
  checked.

**The residual the harness states rather than hides.** A forced disconnect
cancels what was tracked when it started; a task recorded on that server
after it started keeps running once it removes the connection, even if
another teardown owes it (that teardown then finds the connection gone).
This is the residual stated since rev 3 (README, SECURITY: "cancels only
the tasks pmcp still tracks when it starts"). Main also leaves such a task
running, but its `cancel_active_tasks` goes through `cancel_task` and so
reports the task as an error (not found, or not connected); rev 5 made the
teardown skip an entry whose connection is gone instead of erroring, and the
board accepted that skip in round 4. So the task left running is not new;
that it is skipped rather than reported dates from rev 5. The
harness found it on its first run (`DFTSST`) and exempts exactly those
tasks, by its own model, only when the disconnect removed the connection.
Closing it would need a disconnect to keep cancelling until no teardown owes
anything on the connection, with new teardowns able to start during its own
awaits: an unbounded wait, out of scope for this issue.

**Red runs** (rev 12's module on earlier `src`, on `/var/tmp`):
- rev 11 (`8ddb584`): 17 failed, 3 errors, all rev 12 tests -- both
  falsifiers, claude's variant, all six overlap alphabets that reach the
  defect (for example `two-forced+plain FDFDSd`: `beta::f1-1` owed and never
  cancelled), the handoff loop (custody 100: no inheritance), the restore,
  connection-filter and dedupe tests, and the guards;
- rev 12 before the restore (`451bdaa`, readers unified, no restore): 9
  failed, 1 error -- codex's falsifier (`([None, None], None)`), the three
  abort alphabets (`abort+get STFGAD`: `beta::b1` owed and never cancelled;
  `abort+get+cancel-later FGTSSA`: `beta::f1-0` dropped while tracked), the
  restore tests and the guards.

**Mutants M101–M113:** `get_tracked_tasks` blind to custodies; the
connection filter dropped; the disconnect refusal gate, the teardown
snapshot, the refresh gate and `mcp_tasks_remaining` each reading the
registry alone; `owed_records` repeating what the registry holds; a pin that
does not restore; a restore of another connection's record; a restore before
the pin; a restore that skips the count or the caps; releasing a custody
that drops its records. M96–M98 (the teardown's custody fallback) are
retired with the code; M27, M28, M49, M83, M89 and M100 are re-pointed.

## Changes

| File | Kind | Lines (spike) |
|---|---|---|
| `src/pmcp/client/task_registry.py` | create | +373 (rev 9: pins and `_apply_caps`; rev 10: the bound in caps and requests; rev 11: `TaskCustody`, `watch`/`unwatch`/`held`, `_evict` hands an owed record to its custody, the two bounds in live requests; rev 12: `owed_records`, `restore`, the bound per server in `R(s)`, the obligation bound) |
| `src/pmcp/client/manager.py` | modify | +449 / −103: `_tasks = TaskRegistry()`; `drop_server`; `_record_task` calls `put`; `_evict_terminal_tasks`, `_max_terminal_tasks` and `_record_order` removed; `_terminal_task` delegates to `task_is_terminal`; rev 2: `_cancel_tracked_for_teardown`, used by `disconnect_server` and `cancel_active_tasks`; `get_task_result` reads its context once; rev 3: `_send_task_cancel`, the one sender, split out of `cancel_task`; the teardown helper cancels an evicted snapshot task; the `_remove_server_indexes` docstring is made exact for remote servers; rev 4: the teardown's context precedence; `_task_client_unavailable`, shared with `_task_client`, and the no-live-connection skip; rev 5: `_record_task`'s `requestor_context` required (keyword-only, no default); `_record_reply` and `_fetch_task` (split from `get_task`), and `_send_task_cancel`, carry the held context into every post-await record; rev 5 (codex F001): `ManagedClient.connection_id`; `_record_task(..., connection=)` required, and a replaced connection's reply is not stored; `_task_client(server, *, bound_to)` refuses a record from a previous connection; the teardown checks the snapshot's binding first; rev 6: `TaskReply`, `call_tool_with_task` and `get_task_result_with_task` (the old names are thin wrappers); rev 7: `_owns`; `_record_task` decides ownership before any registry read and merges only a same-connection record; `_fetch_task(connection=)` resolves nothing; the `get_task_result` fallback runs on the captured connection, and only while it is the server's; rev 8: `_record_task(..., requestor_context, fallback_context)`: supplied, else the existing same-connection record's, else the captured fallback; rev 9: `_pins_task` and `_pinned`; `fallback_context`, `stored_context` and `_record_reply` removed; the teardown pins its snapshot and sends each record's current context; rev 10: `_teardown_locks`; the teardown takes server names and reads its snapshot under the lock; `_pinned` releases through `ExitStack`; rev 11: `_teardown_locks` removed; the teardown reads every server's snapshot in its first statement, holds it in `_custody`, and pins only the entry whose cancel is in flight; `_record_task` merges with a custody's record when the registry no longer holds the key; rev 12: `get_tracked_tasks` is the one source (registry plus `owed_records` on the current connection, `_on_current_connection`); `_pinned` restores a custody's record for the request that pins it; the teardown's custody fallback removed |
| `src/pmcp/tools/handlers.py` | modify (rev 2) | +38 / −28: `refresh` reports a failed forced disconnect; rev 6: `invoke`, `tasks_result` and `tasks_list` use the record built from the reply, never a lookup after the await; `invoke`'s default redaction is main's rule |

## Documentation impact

### The three #337 CHANGELOG corrections, each checked against the code

1. **"A `NaN` inside a tool result now reaches the caller as a `NaN` token."**
   The issue says this applies to every relayed downstream value. I
   measured it on the spike (`nan_relay.py`, reproduced
   below). The script uses the real `ClientManager` and `GatewayTools`, puts
   a `NaN` in each reply, and serialises the output with the gateway's own
   encoder, `json.dumps(result.model_dump(), indent=2)`
   (`server.py:450-467`):

   | Gateway tool | Where the NaN was | `NaN` token in the caller's output |
   |---|---|---|
   | `gateway.invoke` | tool result | **yes** |
   | `gateway.tasks_result` | `result` | **yes** |
   | `gateway.tasks_get` | task `raw` | no (`raw` is nulled, as #337's entry says) |
   | `gateway.tasks_list` | task `raw` | no |

   Resource reads (`server.py:618-630`) and prompt gets (`:688-702`) pass on
   only `uri`, `mimeType` and `text` strings, so no number from the
   downstream reaches the caller there.

   So "not just tool results" is right, but "every relayed value" is too
   broad. The corrected text names both paths that relay a value as sent,
   and the two that do not.
2. **"a string where a number is expected"**. Measured with
   `_task_info_from_payload`:
   - `ttl: "5"` and `pollInterval: "5"` are unusable;
   - `createdAt: "5"`, `createdAt: "2026-01-02T03:04:05Z"`,
     `lastUpdatedAt: "5.5"` and the same ISO string are **kept**: 5.0,
     1767323045.0, 5.5 and 1767323045.0;
   - `lastUpdatedAt: "soon"` is unusable.

   The source is `_usable_task_timestamp` (`types.py:563-596`): "a numeric
   string or an ISO 8601 string". The bullet becomes: a string for `ttl` or
   `pollInterval` (a numeric or ISO 8601 string is still accepted for
   `createdAt` and `lastUpdatedAt`/`updatedAt`).
3. **"now reports `updatedAt: null`"**. `McpTaskInfo` declares `updated_at`
   and no alias (`types.py:633`). A recorded task with an unusable
   `lastUpdatedAt` dumps the key `updated_at` with value `None`, and `raw`
   keeps the wire key `lastUpdatedAt` (measured). The text becomes
   `updated_at: null`.

The #337 bullet on eviction ("Finished tasks past the 100-record cap are now
evicted in the order pmcp last recorded them …") stays true. It gains one
sentence pointing to the #338 entry, which follows it in the same
`### Changed` section. That section is still under `[Unreleased]`.

### The new entry, README and SECURITY

These are given verbatim in the patch. Each states the bound (100 per server,
1000 in total) and the victim order. The entries also cover what happens to
an evicted task (`tasks_get` tracks it again; cancel needs that first; a
disconnect neither waits for it nor cancels it; send `requestor_context`
again) and the fact that `tasks_list` still returns everything listed.
`python3 scripts/check_security_claims.py` stays `OK` (129 cited node ids).

## Dependencies & order

1. Independent of every open plan. It applies to `31c1357` as is.
2. It **composes with Consiliency/pmcp#335** (NaN in relayed results). That
   plan may reword correction 1 again; the text here is what the code does on
   `31c1357`.
3. Within this plan: write the registry, then switch `manager.py` to it, then
   migrate the 7 test sites, then add the new module, then the docs.

## Verification

Run from a fresh worktree of `origin/main` on dev0 (a team host).

```bash
git -C ~/code/pmcp worktree add -b fix/338-task-cap "$WORKTREE_ROOT/pmcp-338-fix" origin/main
cd "$WORKTREE_ROOT/pmcp-338-fix"
uv sync --all-extras -p 3.10
UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=.venv-3.11 uv sync --all-extras -p 3.11
UV_PROJECT_ENVIRONMENT=.venv-3.12 uv sync --all-extras -p 3.12
mkdir -p /var/tmp/pmcp-338-bt-$USER
```

Apply *Verbatim bodies* (How to apply), then:

```bash
BT=/var/tmp/pmcp-338-bt-$USER
REL="tests/test_task_tracking_cap.py tests/test_task_numeric_bounds.py tests/test_client_manager.py \
     tests/test_tools.py tests/test_phase4_e2e.py tests/test_gateway_tool_schemas.py \
     tests/test_integration.py tests/test_lazy_start.py tests/test_scoped_advisor_audit.py"
# 1. CI gates
.venv/bin/ruff check src/ tests/ && .venv/bin/ruff format --check src/ tests/
.venv/bin/mypy src/pmcp --exclude baml_client
.venv/bin/python scripts/check_security_claims.py
# 2. the relevant suites on 3.10, 3.11 and 3.12 (npm vars unset: dev0 needs it for test_tools)
for v in .venv .venv-3.11 .venv-3.12; do
  env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir $v/bin/python -m pytest $REL -q -p no:cacheprovider --basetemp=$BT/rel${v#.venv}
done
# 3. the full suite, once
env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir .venv/bin/python -m pytest -q -p no:cacheprovider --basetemp=$BT/full
# 4. the mutation table (Verbatim bodies), from the tree root
env -u npm_config_cache -u npm_config_store_dir .venv/bin/python -u mutants.py "$PWD"
```

## Acceptance criteria

- [ ] One server's records never evict another's below the total: A listing
  98, 99, 100 or 500 leaves B's two finished tasks
  (`test_one_server_listing_tasks_never_evicts_another_servers`).
- [ ] A server keeps at most 100 records, finished and unfinished, for
  `working`, `input_required`, and every status #298 turns into `null`
  (`test_an_unfinished_flood_is_capped_at_the_newest_100`, 6 cases).
- [ ] Victims are finished before unfinished, each in record order. A re-record
  is the newest, and a status change moves a record between classes (5
  eviction-order tests).
- [ ] The total is 1000, and it takes from the largest server, with ties
  broken by the oldest victim, never the new record (3 tests). The registry
  agrees with a list-scan restatement over 3000 random puts across 5 cap
  pairs.
- [ ] Every record path in the derived table goes through the cap
  (`test_every_record_path_is_capped`, 7 cases). The set of `_record_task`
  callers equals that table (`test_record_task_is_called_by_exactly_the_tested_paths`).
- [ ] Only `_record_task` builds a record or calls `put`, and the registry
  cannot be written around `put` or rebound (2 structural tests).
- [ ] `gateway.invoke` at the cap keeps its record and redacts by default.
  `gateway.tasks_list` still returns all 500 listed tasks.
- [ ] An evicted unfinished task: `tasks_cancel` says not found, `tasks_get`
  tracks it again, and cancel then succeeds.
- [ ] A disconnect drops only that server's records and frees its share. A
  reconcile keeps them.
- [ ] **F001, rev 3 outcome:** a record evicted during a forced teardown is
  still cancelled, for both triggers, by its id with its stored
  `requestor_context`.
  - The forced disconnect returns `(True, 0, None)`.
  - `cancel_active_tasks` returns `(2, [])`.
  - Restart, the gateway disconnect and a forced refresh all succeed.
  - `tasks/cancel` is sent for `['a', 'b']`, as on main.

  That is 5 sites × 2 triggers. Two more cases: a task finished meanwhile is
  counted with nothing sent, and an evicted task whose server is gone is
  skipped.
- [ ] **Round-10 F001 (rev 11):**
  - A forced teardown cancels every task tracked and unfinished on its
    servers when it was requested: its snapshot is its first statement, with
    no await before it (structural), and an owed task the cap evicts while it
    waits is cancelled from its custody, with the newest context pmcp held.
    Codex's falsifier passes as written: `beta::b` is cancelled, `(2, [])`.
  - After every `put`, `unpin` and `watch`, with R the requests in flight at
    that instant (the sum of the pin counts, never more than the requests
    the test has in flight): a server holds at most `max(per_server, R(s) + 1)`
    records and the registry at most `max(total, R + 1)`. Codex's
    shrinking-R ordering retains 100 records with R = 0.
  - A custody is its request-time snapshot, never larger, and it is
    released however the teardown ends (rev 12 replaces its size bound with
    the obligation bound: see round 11).
  - All 180 orderings × {forced refresh of two servers, forced disconnect}
    pass the live bounds, cancel every owed task, and send the context an
    independent model expects; codex's 1,001 handoffs peak at most 101; the
    12 overlap variants pass the live bounds.
  - No teardown lock (round-10 N1 and N2), and a teardown pins one entry at a
    time.
- [ ] **Round-11 codex F001 (rev 12):** a request that pins a task held
  only in a custody puts it back in the registry, pinned first, on the
  current connection only; codex's falsifier passes as written (both polls
  and the later cancel carry `{'tenant': 'b'}`); `get_task_record` is read
  only under a pin; in the overlap harness's abort, poll and re-track
  alphabets every request carries the model's context and nothing tracked is
  dropped.
- [ ] **Round-11 claude/grok F001 (rev 12):**
  - Every reader of active tasks sees a task a running teardown owes after
    the cap moved it into a custody, on its server's current connection
    only: the teardown snapshot, both disconnect paths, the refresh gate and
    `mcp_tasks_remaining`. Structurally: the registry and the custodies are
    read only through `get_tracked_tasks` (plus `get_task_record` and the
    merge).
  - The seat's falsifier passes as written (`b1` is cancelled), and its
    non-forced variant refuses, then the refresh cancels `b1` with its
    context.
  - All 2,880 orderings of the overlap harness: no owed task is left without a
    `tasks/cancel` (except the stated rev 3 residual, by the harness's own
    model), no non-forced disconnect succeeds while a task runs, every cancel
    carries the newest context, and the bounds hold after every operation.
  - The registry bound is unchanged. The tasks tracked only through
    custodies never exceed the owed evictions of the current chain; codex's
    handoff loop gives a registry peak of 101 and a newest custody of exactly
    1,100.
  - The mutation runner counts a kill only for pytest exit 1 with a failing
    test named, and every mutant in the table is killed under that rule.
- [ ] **Round-9 F001 (rev 10, as amended by rev 11):** overlapping forced
  teardowns cannot stack retention (codex's handoff loop: a peak of at most
  101), and one failing release leaks nothing. Rev 10's lock and its N1 skip
  are superseded by rev 11.
- [ ] **Round-8 F001 (rev 9):**
  - A record with a request in flight is never evicted (rev 11: a
    teardown's request is its one in-flight cancel).
  - The newest context wins in every order, including eviction after the
    newer call (the seat's falsifier, plus 90 generated cases).
  - Every `put` and `unpin` keeps
    `len(s) <= max(per_server, pinned_s + 1)` and
    `len <= max(total, pinned + 1)` (rev 11 states this in live requests:
    see round 10).
  - Every pin is released on reply, exception, cancellation and timeout
    (16 cases, plus the autouse leak guard).
  - `pin`/`unpin` happen only in `_pinned`, and every capturing request is
    pinned.
  - `fallback_context` and the `stored_context` chain are removed (rev 11
    restores the teardown's "gone from the registry" path, as a cancel from
    the custody).
- [ ] **Round-7 F001 (rev 8):** a reply never replaces a newer context on
  the same connection. Supplied wins, then the existing record's, then the
  captured fallback (codex's falsifier, plus 20 generated cases).
  `CONTEXT_FLOW` pins both contexts at every `_record_task` call (13). The
  resolution guard follows plain helpers, with `_owns` as the only
  exception (5 fixture cases).
- [ ] **Round-6 F001/F002 (rev 7):** a late reply from a replaced connection
  takes nothing from the replacement. Its result fallback is never sent to
  the replacement (codex's two falsifiers). Every manager task operation ×
  reply path × {tracked, evicted}, with a replacement registered mid-await,
  sends nothing to it, records nothing from it, and merges nothing from it
  (14 generated cases). No request function resolves a client after its
  first await (8 derived; 2 per-iteration).
- [ ] **Round-5 F001 (rev 6):** `gateway.invoke`, `gateway.tasks_result` and
  `gateway.tasks_list` report the reply's own task, never a same-id task a
  replacement connection tracks (3 falsifiers). No function in `src/pmcp`
  reads task state after an await unless it is listed in `READ_AFTER_AWAIT`
  (15). Every `_send_request` method is classified (9), and every `tasks/…`
  string is a bound send. Every write to `_clients` is classified (9), and
  registrations really drop first. `gateway.invoke` redacts by default only
  when a task was requested.
- [ ] **Codex F001 (rev 5):** no stored task id or context is ever sent on a
  connection other than the one it came from. On codex's falsifier the
  replacement receives nothing. The three bound sends refuse a
  previous-connection record, and the teardown skips one. A reply from a
  replaced connection is not recorded (5 paths). Every `tasks/*` send (4)
  and binding (12) matches its table. Every registration drops the name's
  records first (3). `gateway.invoke` redacts an unrecorded task reply.
- [ ] **Round-4 F001 (rev 5):** `_record_task`'s `requestor_context`, and
  the held context of `_record_reply`, `_fetch_task` and `_send_task_cancel`,
  are keyword-only with no default. Every call in `src/pmcp` matches
  `CONTEXT_FLOW` (10). On every post-await record path (5), a task evicted
  during the await keeps its context, and the next cancel sends it. That
  includes the seat's falsifier. A reply naming another task does not take
  this task's context.
- [ ] **Round-3 F001:** a task evicted mid-teardown and then re-tracked
  without a context is cancelled with the context pmcp held (the seat's
  falsifier, plus the third trigger on 6 sites). Every read of a stored
  context is classified (5).
- [ ] A snapshot task whose server has no live connection (removed or
  reconnecting, tracked or not) is skipped without raising (3 cases).
- [ ] Every read-then-await site in `src/pmcp` is in `SNAPSHOT_SITES` (14).
  The scan counts any reference to a read method, and transitively any
  reference to a sync helper that reads: aliases, bound methods, `getattr`
  strings, and the seat's helper (9 fixture forms). The sync readers are
  pinned (5). `tasks/cancel` is sent only by
  `_send_task_cancel`, and the teardowns call only the helper.
- [ ] A forced refresh that cannot disconnect a server returns `ok: false`
  with `Server '<name>' could not be disconnected: <reason>`.
- [ ] `get_task_result`'s fallback keeps the stored `requestor_context` when
  the record is evicted mid-call; `get_task` and `cancel_task` record the
  reply.
- [ ] `TaskRegistry` gives `KeyError` (`get` → `None`, `in` → `False`) for
  any key that is not a 2-tuple.
- [ ] The three CHANGELOG corrections read as in *Documentation impact*.
- [ ] ruff, ruff format, mypy and `check_security_claims.py` are clean. The
  relevant suites pass on 3.10, 3.11 and 3.12, and the full suite passes once.
- [ ] Every mutant in the table is killed.
- [ ] `scripts/check_plan_consistency.py` on this file reports no blocking
  inconsistency.

## Mutation table

Measured on the rev 12 spike `451bdaa` with `mutants.py`, run from `/var/tmp` with sha256-verified restores (in *Verbatim
bodies*), in a separate scratch worktree. Each run applies one string replacement and runs five
modules with `-x` (seven since rev 6), with the npm variables unset (rev 7
also stops the run if a restore does not match its saved bytes):
- `tests/test_task_tracking_cap.py`;
- `tests/test_task_numeric_bounds.py`;
- `tests/test_client_manager.py`;
- `tests/test_tools.py`;
- `tests/test_phase4_e2e.py`;
- `tests/test_integration.py` and `tests/test_lazy_start.py` (rev 6).

No pattern missed: the runner prints `PATTERN COUNT` for a replacement that
does not match exactly once, and the log has none.

It then restores the file from a copy saved in memory, never through `git`.

**The kill rule (rev 12, round-11 nit).** A mutant counts as killed only
when pytest exits 1 *and* reports a `FAILED tests/…` or `ERROR tests/…`
line. A collection error (2), an internal error (3), a usage error (4) or a
crash is reported as `NOT KILLED (exit N)`. The whole table was re-run under
this rule: **102 of 102 killed**, one clean run alone on its tree, no `PATTERN COUNT`
misses, no restore failures. Rev 12 adds M101–M113, retires M96–M98 (the
teardown's custody fallback, removed), and re-points M27, M28, M49, M78,
M83, M87, M89 and M100 at the restructured teardown and `_pinned`. The
"first failure" column below is that run's. (An earlier rev 12 run found
M78 and M87 as `PATTERN COUNT 0` after `_pinned` gained the restore; they
were re-pointed and the whole table re-run.)

Rev 11's run, under the old rule: **all 92 killed, in one clean run, alone on its tree** (a `git archive`
of the spike with its own venv on `/var/tmp`), with no `PATTERN COUNT`
misses and no restore failures. Rev 11 adds M89–M100 (M92 is unused),
re-points M27, M28, M28b, M49, M66, M66b and M83 at the custody teardown,
and retires M86 (the lock) and M88 (the N1 `assert`) with the code they
targeted. Rev 10 had added M86–M88 and re-pointed M25, M26, M28, M28b, M49
and M78.

The first rev 11 run had one survivor, M95 (an eviction reaches only the
first custody). The custody test's two snapshots happened to hold the same
object as the evicted record, so the mutant changed nothing it observed. The
test now changes `t` after both snapshots and asserts that both custodies
hold the evicted record; it kills M95, and the table is the clean re-run.

**Behavioural pass.** Every mutant from M46 on was re-run with the nine
structural tests deselected; rev 10 adds the teardown-lock guard. **36 of
42 are still killed.** The 6 survivors are rev 7's, with the same argument:
M49, M68 and M70 are equivalent under the ordering, and M63, M64 and M71 are
guard mutants. M86 (unserialised teardowns) was killed behaviourally by
codex's handoff loop; rev 11 retires it with the lock. Rev 11's and rev
12's teardown and custody mutants (M27, M83, M89–M91, M93–M95, M99–M113)
are all killed with every structural test deselected, 23 of 23, under the
strict rule, re-run on their own tree for rev 12 (`-k "not
(registry_is_written or inline_readers or stored_context or
snapshot_then_await or snapshot_scan or record_write_passes or
bound_to_the_connection or looked_up_after_an_await or replacement_drops or
resolves_its_connection or pins_are_taken or fixes_what_it_owes or
resolution_guard or record_task_is_called or only_record_task or
cannot_be_written or one_sender or every_reader_of_task_state)"`). Two of
those kills are incidental, not semantic: M106 and M107 fail in
`test_tools.py` only because its client-manager double has no `_tasks`.
They are bound by the structural reader guard alone, as their rows say.

**M2, an equivalent mutant, dropped.** M2 makes a per-server overflow evict
from `_largest_server` instead of from the overflowing server. It survived,
and it is equivalent:
- When a bucket overflows, it holds `per_server + 1` records. Every other
  bucket holds at most `per_server`, so the overflowing server *is* the
  largest.
- The two differ only if something lowers `per_server` below an existing
  bucket's size. Tests can; nothing in `src/` changes the caps after
  construction.
